Anthropic Goes Public, Nvidia in Crisis, Markets Tank, Open Source Crackdown?

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·76:41·807.6K views·2026-11-20
Chamath PalihapitiyaCo-Host, All-In Podcast Jensen HuangFormer CEO, Nvidia
TL;DRAnthropic’s record-shattering $2 trillion IPO ($107 billion raised) and Palo Alto Networks' $4.5 billion AI acquisition signal a new era where 9 to 10 vertically integrated "dirt-to-application" megacorps will dominate the market.
Meanwhile, Nvidia has shed $2 trillion in market cap following Jensen Huang’s ouster, as the panel clashes over macro headwinds versus 500x AI token growth and blasts a potential White House crackdown on Chinese open-source AI models. The 10 Megacorps: Chamath Palihapitiya...

TL;DR

Anthropic’s record-shattering $2 trillion IPO ($107 billion raised) and Palo Alto Networks' $4.5 billion AI acquisition signal a new era where 9 to 10 vertically integrated "dirt-to-application" megacorps will dominate the market. Meanwhile, Nvidia has shed $2 trillion in market cap following Jensen Huang’s ouster, as the panel clashes over macro headwinds versus 500x AI token growth and blasts a potential White House crackdown on Chinese open-source AI models.

Chamath’s "Dirt-to-Application" Megacorp Theory

  • The 10 Megacorps: Chamath Palihapitiya argued that AI will create 9 to 10 vertically integrated conglomerates over the next decade that own the entire stack—from land, power, and data centers ("the dirt") to distributed silicon, models, and applications.
    • Examples include Anthropic, OpenAI, SpaceX, Cursor, and Meta.
    • Palo Alto Networks' Signal: CEO Nikesh Arora’s $4.5 billion acquisition of an open-source AI model company (Applied Compute) was rewarded by the market (stock up 8–9% intraday, up 5.86% on the day of taping) because it showed a willingness to break out of its lane and build a full-stack moat.
  • The Death of Buybacks: Chamath and Jason Calacanis agreed that stock buybacks are now a "tell" that management lacks the vision to invest in a high-disruption era.
    • Over the last 10 years, Apple reduced its share count by 34%, Alphabet by 12%, and Meta by 11%. Nvidia authorized $150 billion in additional buybacks in September (bringing total buybacks to $235 billion) prior to Jensen Huang's ouster.
    • Cash Flow Over Labels: Markets will stop categorizing companies as "software" vs. "services" and instead reward pure numerical performance—specifically high gross margins (e.g., 99%) and high free cash flow conversion (e.g., 70%).

Anthropic’s $2T IPO and the AI Bull/Bear Debate

  • Record-Breaking Debut: Anthropic went public at a $2 trillion valuation, raising over $107 billion (surpassing SpaceX’s $85 billion IPO) with its green shoe filled. Shares were up 18% from the IPO price on day one.
    • Financials & Penetration: Anthropic is on a ~$80B–$100B revenue run rate (trading at ~20x–30x top line) with less than 1% of its addressable market penetrated.
  • The Bull Case (Friedberg & Calacanis):
    • Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI): David Friedberg noted that Google (which released 4 models in 2 weeks) and likely Anthropic have hit an RSI runaway loop, driving down the cost per unit of intelligence and creating an insurmountable moat.
    • 500x Token Explosion: Jason Calacanis argued that only 10–20% of corporate workers use AI today, whereas startup employees use it 5–7 hours a day. As corporate adoption scales, revenue per employee (e.g., Microsoft trending from $900K toward $2M/employee) will double every three years to $3M–$10M, driving at least 500x token usage.
    • Frontier ROI: Friedberg emphasized that AI’s true 100x–1,000x ROI lies in solving previously intractable scientific and engineering problems (like new cancer drugs), not incremental cost-cutting.
  • The Bear/Macro Case (Chamath):
    • Despite Anthropic’s strength, macro headwinds dominate: the S&P 500 was down 1.39% and the VIX up 10.83% on the day of taping, while AMD dropped 10–12% on a short report alleging chip sales to China.
    • Chamath warned that sticky inflation, geopolitical turmoil, and rising 10-year Treasury yields (more likely to hit 5.5%–6% than 4.5%) leave the market in a "show-me" state where risk-off positioning makes sense.

White House Open-Source Crackdown

  • Proposed Disclosure Rule: Reuters reported that the White House—led by China Hawks including Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent—is weighing a rule forcing U.S. companies to disclose on their websites if they use Chinese open-source AI models (e.g., DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi). Earlier this year, the House Select Committee on China sent letters to Airbnb, DoorDash, and Cursor over their use of open models.
  • Panel Pushback:
    • Chamath: Called the proposed disclosure a "not retarded label," arguing that smart investors want companies using cost-effective open-source models to protect their margins and avoid leaking proprietary data to closed-source labs.
    • Friedberg: Condemned anti-open-source efforts as a "path of tyranny" that would create regulated monopolies and an Orwellian state. He noted that open-weight models are just data (a CSV file), not runtime executables, and can be freely audited—just like Apache, Mozilla/Firefox, and Android (which powers 70% of phones).
  • Why China Leads Open Source: The panel attributed U.S. lag in open source (Nvidia’s Nemotron, Reflections Beam, New Spark trailing DeepSeek, Kimi, and French Mistral) to distorted VC incentives and extreme talent compensation—citing $40 million ($10M/year over 4 years) equity packages for undergrads at Anthropic and $1 billion recruiting offers at Meta.

Nvidia’s $2T Collapse and Jensen Huang’s Ouster

  • Market Bloodbath: Nvidia has shed $2 trillion in market cap over the last 30 days (down 35% overall, and down 20% in the two weeks since Jensen Huang resigned/was ousted), despite reporting that quarterly revenue and net profit both doubled to $116 billion and $65 billion, respectively.
  • Acting CEO Colette Kress’s Pivot: Kress stated the board will not reinstate Huang, ended H200 chip sales to China (even though Commerce approved them in January; Rubin and Blackwell remain banned), and closed the "Bank of Jensen" by promising "more disciplined and selective" neo-cloud financing.
  • Legal & Talent Crisis: Nvidia faces an SEC probe, class-action lawsuits, and a DOJ investigation into Mega Speed. Employees are signing petitions, and Travis Kalanick (alongside Antonio Gracias) called for an employee walkout ("Leather jackets for Jensen").
  • Refusal to Speculate: Both Chamath and Friedberg refused to take a stance on Huang’s ouster or potential return, noting that no official facts have been released by the company or regulators and that Nvidia faces high-stakes, closed-door congressional hearings on December 1 and 2.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • 9 to 10 AI Megacorps: "I think that there are going to be nine to 10 mega corps that get built over the next decade because of AI... each mega corp is going to go from the application layer all the way down to the dirt." — *Chamath Palihapitiya*
  • Anthropic as a Multibagger: "If you're willing to buy the stock and own it for five plus years, you could hold a multibagger from here at this entry point. And that's true for them, it's true for SpaceX, it's true for potentially Google even at this moment." — *David Friedberg*
  • 500x Token Usage & Doubling Revenue Per Employee: "We're going to see the number of people in the organizations use it... go 5, 6, 7X... What that means is at least 500 times the token usage is what's going to happen... Every three years, you're going to see the amount of revenue per employee double." — *Jason Calacanis*
  • 10-Year Yield & Market Risk: "I think that it's more likely that the 10 year is at 5 and 1/2 or 6% than it is at 4 and 1/2%." — *Chamath Palihapitiya*
  • U.S. Open-Source Catch-Up: "We'll sit here in a year and the US ones [New Spark, Nemotron, Reflections Beam] will be somewhere in between DeepSeek and Kimmy." — *Jason Calacanis*

Notable Quotes

"The return on dollars spent on tokens or whatever is some small number when you're doing it to produce the same stuff versus it being a much bigger number if you use it to produce things that humans have never produced before, like some new cancer drug or some new material science." — *David Friedberg*
"This continues to be the path of tyranny and any effort to try and curb open source, restrict open source, label open source, and give the government the tools to create effectively regulated monopolies... is going to both limit human prosperity, limit individual liberty, [and] create an Orwellian state." — *David Friedberg*

Anthropic Hits $2 Trillion in Historic IPO | Altimeter's Four Seasons of AI

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(79.9K)·51:35·6.7K views·2026-11-20
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Apoorv AgrawalAI Partner, Altimeter
TL;DRAltimeter AI Partner Apoorv Agrawal breaks down the historic scale of the AI supercycle—where Big Tech CapEx has hit $860 billion—and explains why Anthropic’s S-1 reveals one of the most capital-efficient, fastest-scaling businesses in history, underpinned by a 1.5T–2T IPO valuation (11–14x forward revenue) and a "Four Seasons of AI" framework led by Anthropic, OpenAI, SpaceX, and Google.
Historic Capital Expenditure: Big Tech AI CapEx has reached ~$860 billion this year, having more than tripled over the last two years to rival U.S. defense spending. As a percentage of GDP, this spend already exceeds every major infrastructure boom of the past 100 years...

TL;DR: Altimeter AI Partner Apoorv Agrawal breaks down the historic scale of the AI supercycle—where Big Tech CapEx has hit $860 billion—and explains why Anthropic’s S-1 reveals one of the most capital-efficient, fastest-scaling businesses in history, underpinned by a 1.5T–2T IPO valuation (11–14x forward revenue) and a "Four Seasons of AI" framework led by Anthropic, OpenAI, SpaceX, and Google.

The AI Supercycle & Early-Inning Adoption

  • Historic Capital Expenditure: Big Tech AI CapEx has reached ~$860 billion this year, having more than tripled over the last two years to rival U.S. defense spending. As a percentage of GDP, this spend already exceeds every major infrastructure boom of the past 100 years (railroads, oil & gas, telco, shale).
  • The AI Stack vs. Cloud Economy: While the traditional cloud economy generates ~$600B in application/software revenue supported by ~$300B in infra and ~$80B in semis, the AI economy currently generates ~$140B–$150B at the model/app layer on top of a much larger semiconductor base. However, over the last 2–3 years, the AI economy has grown 6x overall while the apps and models layer has grown ~100x.
  • Shift to Agentic AI: The market has transitioned from "Phase 1" (chatbots/knowledge retrieval, launched with ChatGPT in Nov 2022, making up ~80% of early use cases) to "Phase 2" (agentic AI, long-running agents, and "swarms of agents" for coding, sales, marketing, support, and investment teams, now ~80% of agentic use cases).
  • Widening Adoption Gaps ("A Tale of Two Cities"):
    • Enterprise: OpenAI data shows its top 10% of customers grew usage 17x over the past 1–1.5 years, compared to 2x for the median customer.
    • Individual: Cursor data shows the top 10% of users consume two-thirds of all tokens.
    • Headroom: Fortune 500 customers spending over $100,000 annually on Anthropic report that it represents less than 0.1% of their total expenses, signaling massive runway as adoption moves from 0.1% toward 1%.

Anthropic’s S-1 & IPO Valuation

  • Unprecedented Scale & Growth: Anthropic has grown 10x every year for the last three years, hitting ~$17 billion in Q3 revenue—the fastest scale-up in history.
  • Diversified Enterprise Base: The company has ~12,000 customers paying over $100,000/year and ~1,900 customers paying over $1 million/year, with 900% net revenue retention and minimal customer concentration.
  • Capital Efficiency & Margins: Anthropic burned less than $10 billion in cash to reach its ~$17B revenue run rate. The cost to serve $1 of revenue has dropped from $0.88 of compute to $0.15, yielding ~85% margins.
  • Compute Monetization: Through heterogeneous compute orchestration (TPUs, Trainium, Nvidia GPUs), Anthropic’s compute monetization has risen from ~$16/watt to ~$60/watt.
  • Valuation Thesis: At the rumored $1.5T–$2T IPO range, Anthropic trades at 11x–14x forward GAAP revenue—a reasonable multiple compared to current market comps.

The "Four Seasons of AI" & Evergreen Open Source

  • OpenAI: Now at over 1.2 billion ChatGPT users, OpenAI ended September at a ~$50B revenue run rate (beating year-end guidance). Following a "code red" earlier in the year, it shipped frontier models (Astra) and products (Codex, ChatGPT Work) while upgrading its commercial leadership (Dolly Rojik, Brian McCarthy, Chad Peets).
  • SpaceX (AI Infrastructure): SpaceX’s ~$60B revenue run rate is now ~50% AI infrastructure (SI unit), ~33% Starlink, and the remainder launch. Its SI unit added over 20% relative market share in Q3.
    • Spot Compute Model: Unlike neoclouds (CoreWeave, Nebius) that lease long-term (1–3 years) at $10–$20/watt, SpaceX rents compute at spot prices ($25–$50/watt, currently at the $50/watt high end), allowing it to recoup CapEx in under a year (vs. 2–3 years for neoclouds).
    • Scale: Estimated to end this year at ~3 GW of compute, guiding to ~13 GW by the end of next year.
  • Google ("A Tale of Two Cities"): While Google Search (~$250B run rate) is growing at 12%, Google Cloud—which houses its TPU business—is at a $120B revenue run rate growing 103% YoY (8x faster than Search), serving customers like Anthropic and Oracle.
  • Open Source (The Evergreen Fifth Season): Expected to capture 20%–30% of all AI spend, driven by AI-native startups (e.g., Cursor, Instinct, Decagon, Harvey) and enterprises seeking control, lower inference costs, and data sovereignty.
    • Open-source frontier models consistently lag closed frontier models by ~6 months.
    • Value accrues at the inference/cloud layer via platforms like Baseten, Fireworks, and Modal (e.g., OpenAI recently partnered with Baseten so enterprise customers can burn down OpenAI credits on open-source models).

Data Center Bottlenecks & Market Outlook

  • Power & Permitting Constraints: The U.S. is expected to end this year at 35–40 GW of compute capacity online. While 40–50 GW of additions were forecasted for next year, permitting and regulation will likely cause a 10–15 GW shortfall.
  • Winners vs. Losers: Hyperscalers holding large capacity pools benefit as bottlenecks, though their expansion ambitions face risk. Conversely, smaller neoclouds and startups "17th in line" for capacity face existential risk.
  • Who Wins the Stack: While "everyone is going to win" (chips, data centers, clouds, models), it has so far been a "game of emperors" favoring the largest balance sheets. Over time, improving compute efficiency will allow more value to accrue to the application and startup layers.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • OpenAI Revenue: OpenAI will generate $140B–$150B in GAAP revenue next year (up from ~$20B–$21B at the end of last year) — *Apoorv Agrawal*
  • SpaceX Revenue & Compute: SpaceX is guiding to a $100B revenue run rate by the end of this year and $500B–$1T by the end of next year, alongside scaling from ~3 GW of compute this year to ~13 GW by the end of next year — *Apoorv Agrawal (citing SpaceX guidance)*
  • U.S. Compute Shortfall: Permitting and regulatory bottlenecks will likely cause a 10–15 GW shortfall against the 40–50 GW of U.S. compute capacity additions forecasted for next year — *Apoorv Agrawal*
  • Open Source Market Share: Open source will capture 20%–30% of all AI spend long-term, with gross margins accruing primarily at the inference layer (Baseten, Fireworks, Modal) — *Apoorv Agrawal*
  • Anthropic IPO Performance: Anthropic’s IPO (at $1.5T–$2T, or 11–14x forward revenue) will be a "phenomenal short-term success, and certainly long-term," cementing the "private Mag 7" as the leading companies for the next decade and more — *Apoorv Agrawal*

Notable Quotes

"Fortune 500 customers that spend more than $100,000 on Anthropic say that it is less than 0.1% of the total expense. We're in inning one of this whole thing... it's going to go from 0.1% of their expenses to 1% and you know, that's how you get the 10x gains." — *Apoorv Agrawal*
"They burned less than $10 billion of cash [to get to $17B revenue run rate]... Serving a dollar of revenue has gone down from 88 cents of compute to 15 cents. That's why they got 85% margins." — *Apoorv Agrawal*
"So far, it has been a game of emperors and we've seen the biggest players with the biggest balance sheet win. But... as compute efficiency started to kick in, as models get cheaper, more and more value will accrue at the top of the stack, which is models and applications and new startups." — *Apoorv Agrawal*
"We thought SpaceX was a space business, but turns out it's an AI business." — *Apoorv Agrawal*

Huang's Resignation Dissolves the Nvidia AI Vertical Stack | The Weekly Wrap

Steve Eisman(250.0K)·22:11·287.3K views·2026-11-20
Steve EismanHost, The Weekly Wrap Jensen HuangFormer CEO, Nvidia
TL;DRThe resignation of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections have triggered a market correction, effectively dissolving Nvidia’s circularly financed "virtual holding company."
With Nvidia no longer backstopping the AI stack, AI is no longer a single monolithic trade, bringing stock picking and fundamental valuation back to the forefront. Market Action: From Friday through Thursday (Nov 19), the NASDAQ fell ~4%. The market declined Monday and Tuesday,...

TL;DR

The resignation of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections have triggered a market correction, effectively dissolving Nvidia’s circularly financed "virtual holding company." With Nvidia no longer backstopping the AI stack, AI is no longer a single monolithic trade, bringing stock picking and fundamental valuation back to the forefront.

Market Correction & The "Jensen Unwind"

  • Market Action: From Friday through Thursday (Nov 19), the NASDAQ fell ~4%. The market declined Monday and Tuesday, staged a small rally Wednesday, and saw only a brief, fading pop on Thursday following the Anthropic IPO.
  • Political & Leadership Catalysts: A Democratic sweep of both houses in the midterms introduced the risk of an AI moratorium or data center construction slowdown. Combined with Jensen Huang’s resignation, these events dwarf all other market news.
  • S&P 500 AI Concentration:
    • Infotech represents 40% of the S&P 500; adding tech-adjacent giants like Google and Amazon brings it to 50%.
    • Including utilities, large banks, asset managers/alternative asset managers funding data centers, and industrials providing construction or power (e.g., Eaton, Quanta, GE Vernova) pushes AI-exposed companies to 65% of the S&P 500.
  • Circular Financing & Dissolution: Under Huang, Nvidia operated as a "virtual vertical holding company" by taking equity stakes or issuing loans to customers who used that capital to buy Nvidia chips—including Anthropic ($20B), OpenAI ($30B), SpaceX ($20B), CoreWeave ($2.5B), and Nebius ($2B).
    • Huang's resignation dissolves this structure. While tech leaders want him back, Treasury Secretary Bessent has appointed himself watchdog, making a front-door return to Nvidia unlikely.
    • Banks, private credit, private equity, and shareholders must now evaluate these AI companies on their own standalone merits (cash flow, balance sheets, leadership, and financing).

Nvidia’s Earnings & Forward Uncertainty

  • Q3 Results (Backward-Looking): Nvidia reported EPS of $2.68 (+111% YoY) and revenue of $116B (+103% YoY).
  • Guidance & Stock Reaction: Management guided next quarter’s revenue to $130B, explicitly excluding "compute revenue from China." The stock rose ~3% after hours, opened up Wednesday, and closed slightly down.
  • Opacity & Red Flags:
    • Roughly 20% of Nvidia's revenue reportedly came from shipments to "dicey Asian entities" that were likely illegal and could drop to zero.
    • It remains unclear whether Nvidia’s guidance excludes only direct sales to China or also sales to Asian intermediaries funneling chips into China.
    • Eisman argues management made a mistake by issuing guidance at all before unwinding circular financing and quantifying lost revenue.

How to Think About Quarterly Earnings

  • Earnings Cadence: Begins ~15 days after quarter-end, opening with the banks and closing ~6 weeks later with Nvidia.
  • Shifting Sector Metrics:
    • *Banks:* Loan growth, net interest margin (NIM) changes, operating efficiency, and credit quality.
    • *Tech/AI:* Shifted from revenue, earnings, and backlog to CapEx, cash flow, and whether CapEx is funded internally or via capital markets.
    • *Software (SaaS):* Shifted from seat growth and pricing trends to any signs that AI is slowing growth rates.
  • Thesis Checking & Guidance: Earnings serve as a data-point check (e.g., 16% vs. 14% growth against a 15% thesis). Because companies "under-promise and over-deliver" and can massage accounting within limits, missing guidance signals a potential fundamental issue.
  • The "Steve Eisman Drama Scale" (1–10, Nvidia at 11): For low-drama stocks like Coca-Cola, backward-looking numbers suffice. When sector narratives shift rapidly (like AI today, or Cisco’s recent strong report), backward-looking earnings tell investors very little about future quarters.

Retail & Consumer Earnings: Housing Drag vs. Turnarounds

  • Home Depot: Facing the worst existing home sales market in decades ("a bad macro trumps a good company"). Reported EPS of $3.86 (+3% YoY) and same-store sales comp of +2.2% (50 bps beat), but guided Q4 comps to just 1% (below consensus), sending the stock down Tuesday (roughly flat YTD).
  • Lowe’s: Reported EPS of $3.10 (+1% YoY) and same-store sales comp of +1.8% (160 bps beat, but still poor).
  • Target: Turnaround efforts are succeeding, with EPS of $2.65 (+49% YoY) and same-store sales growth of +5.6% (well above expectations).
  • Walmart: Reported EPS of $0.65 (+5% YoY, a $0.01 beat) and net sales growth of +4.4%. Trading as a "high-class safety stock" amid the post-September 30 AI reversal, shares have climbed from $105 to $125.

The Anthropic IPO & Mailbag

  • Anthropic IPO: Went public Thursday (Nov 19) to an anti-climactic reception. Eisman finds the stock uncompelling and is uninvolved due to:
    • *Slowing Growth:* Sequential revenue growth decelerated from 145% in Q2 to 45% in Q3, attributed to the end of "token maxing."
    • *Lack of Moats:* Easy for customers to switch models, forcing Anthropic to cut prices by 50% in October to defend market share against cheaper open-weight models.
    • *Balance Sheet & Cost Risks:* Anthropic has $900B in commitments, meaning it will have to raise expensive debt (a junk credit rating is possible) while spending heavily to keep its frontier models competitive.
  • Mailbag 1 — Correction vs. Bear Market Positioning: A correction lasts 1–2 years; a true bear market lasts years (none since the 1970s). The US economy is too dynamic and banks are too well-capitalized for a long-term bear market.
    • *Long-term investors:* Dollar-cost average into the S&P 500 index and ignore the noise.
    • *Traders:* Rotate into a healthcare index, a staples index, and short-term Treasuries.
  • Mailbag 2 — Identifying "Liar Loans" in 2008: Moody’s securitization database revealed that starting in 2006, ~50% of all securitized subprime loans were "low doc" or "no doc" loans. Underwriters actively cajoled borrowers into overstating income (e.g., telling a $40,000 earner they needed to state $50,000 to qualify), though the exact percentage of liar loans within low-doc pools remains unknown.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "The Jensen unwind is just starting, and it's going to get very ugly." — Steve Eisman
  • "In the end, Nvidia's revenue is going to be lower. How much lower, we don't yet know. Maybe it can grow from there, but it has to go down first, I think." — Steve Eisman
  • "It's going to be several quarters before we know how much Nvidia's business model will be negatively impacted." — Steve Eisman
  • "There is no sign, no sign, that the housing market will improve anytime soon." — Steve Eisman
  • "I don't foresee a long-term bear market because the US economy is more dynamic than perhaps anytime in its history... The US will recover." — Steve Eisman
  • "We're spending $750 to $800 billion of CapEx in 2026 going to a trillion next year [2027]. In 2030, to justify all those numbers... our economy [has] to spend in aggregate per year $2.4 to $3 trillion a year." — Steve Eisman (referencing his Substack interview with Gary Gensler)

Notable Quotes

"Without Nvidia backstopping the AI stack, AI is no longer one trade, and that's why stock picking is back, and valuation is back." — Steve Eisman
"The lesson here is that a bad macro trumps a good company almost every time." — Steve Eisman (on Home Depot)
"Anthropic is a business with slowing revenue growth, increasing expenses and increasing cost of capital. I just think there are too many risks here and that's why I am uninvolved." — Steve Eisman

But first, I will explain how and why the role Nvidia played under Wang's leadership is forever changed and how and why stock picking is back. Infotech represents 40% of the S&P. If you then add companies like Google and Amazon that are techy, but technically not in the Infotech category, you quickly get to 50%. Then, add the utility sector, all the large banks, alternative asset managers and asset managers involved in funding AI data center construction, industrial companies involved in AI data center construction like Eaton, and industrial companies involved in providing the power to AI data centers like Quanta and GE Vernova, and you easily get to 65% of the S&P. In a sense, the entire market has become one AI trade, but it's even more convoluted than that. Jensen Wang did not want to sell all his chips to the hyperscalers. So, in a process called circular financing, he decided to take equity stakes or give loans to a multitude of companies including Anthropic, 20 billion, OpenAI, 30 billion, SpaceX, 20 billion, CoreWeave, 2.5 billion, Nebius, 2 billion, and several others. Essentially, he gave them money, and they used that money to buy his chips. He created, I think, a virtual vertical holding company, and while the party lasted, everybody was happy. And now the party is over, his resignation is the effective dissolution of the virtual holding company. All the pieces he financed are now, more likely than not, going to be on their own, although it's still early to know for sure, but they need to be evaluated on their own merits and not as part of a larger hole. Will they get financing? Are they well run? Who is the leader? What are the projections? What's the cash flow like, the balance sheet, the earnings and the potential? Without Nvidia backstopping the AI stack, AI is no longer one trade, and that's why stock picking is back, and valuation is back. Because valuation is back, I think this is a good time to take a step back and discuss how to think about quarterly earnings. Earnings season begins around 15 days after the end of the quarter. The banks report first and Nvidia closes the session. It's all about 6 weeks long, and there are some days where so many companies report that it can be overwhelming. So how should you think about earnings season and what should you look at? The first thing to realize is that companies report their results differently. Some just put out a fairly simple press release. Others, like the banks, put out an earnings press release, a very large financial supplement, and a conference called deck. Perhaps even more importantly, is to realize that what investors care about differs between sectors and can also change over time. With respect to the banks, for example, investors care about loan growth, changes in the net interest margin, operating efficiency, and changes in credit quality. In tech, investors used to care only about revenue and earnings growth and backlog. However, now that AI dominates, investors also care just as much about CapEx, cash flow, and whether a company is going to fund its CapEx needs out of cash flow or the capital markets. When it comes to software companies, those companies all employ a SaaS model, software as a service. In analyzing that model, investors cared about growth in the number of seats and pricing trends. Today, however, investors are looking for any signs that AI is causing software growth rates to slow down. Think of quarterly earnings as a data point check on your thesis. Suppose your thesis for a company is that revenue will grow at least 15% every quarter for the next several years. If the company reports growth of 16%, then all is good. But if revenue growth slows to 14%, you've got some soul searching to do. Every company makes mistakes. Every company hits stumbling blocks. You're going to have to listen to the conference call and do some research to ascertain whether this quarter was just a hiccup or has the story changed for the worst. That is not an easy decision to make. Also, there is the impact of drama. I call this the Steve Eisman drama scale. It goes from 1 to 10 and in the case of Nvidia, it goes to 11. If you own stock in a company with low drama, like say Coca-Cola, it doesn't get more low drama than that. Then, when Coca-Cola reports, all you need to do is go through the numbers to make sure your thesis is intact. But sometimes, the narrative of a sector or stock changes and the drama scale skyrockets. At that point, quarterly earnings reports become far less important because they are backward looking. Right now, the AI narrative is changing almost daily. It was a hyper growth tale, but because of politics and the Nvidia scandal, AI data center construction and the entire AI story could, I emphasize, could, slow. It could slow a bit or it could slow a lot. It's too early to tell. So when a company like Cisco reported great numbers last week, all that showed you was that AI CapEx was explosive, but that report tells you nothing about future quarters. One more thing to address is why do investors react so poorly when a company misses key metrics, even if they missed them only barely? Investors aren't stupid. They know that within limits, companies have the ability to massage their numbers. The accounting rules aren't completely rigid. Companies give guidance for either the next quarter or the next fiscal year, and when they give that guidance, they have sufficient visibility in their backlog, etc. to render that guidance, and they also know that they are going to beat that guidance. Under promise and over deliver is the mantra for literally every corporation. So when a company doesn't even meet the guidance but actually misses it, something is really wrong. The only question is whether it is a one time blip that will last a quarter or two or something really fundamental and long-term. Now let's turn back to Nvidia and its results. Before we get to those results, I want to point out that they are completely backward looking. The forward look is very complicated. Apparently, 20% of Nvidia's revenue comes from shipments to dicey Asian entities that were probably illegal. Those shipments now could go to zero. Also, assuming that the new management team of Nvidia abandons or curtails all circular financing guarantees, etc., we need to then know how much of the company's revenue comes from that as well, and right now we don't really know. In the end, Nvidia's revenue is going to be lower. How much lower, we don't yet know. Maybe it can grow from there, but it has to go down first, I think. Now, the earnings results were of course, fine. Earnings per share was $2.68 up 111% versus last year. Revenue of 116 billion was up 103%. Nvidia gave guidance for the next quarter of revenue of an amazing 130 billion, and management said that that number does not include, quote, compute revenue from China, unquote. Personally, I think that giving this guidance is a mistake. So much has to be unwound here that the better part of valor would have been to provide no guidance until management has its arms around all its problems. But, I'm sure management thought that if they did not provide guidance then the stock would collapse, and they wanted to avoid that. The stock was up around 3% after hours. Given how much Nvidia is down recently, that was not all that impressive, and it did not hold. Despite opening up on Wednesday, the stock closed down slightly. By the way, the key sentence, I'm going back to it, in the press release was that Nvidia gave revenue guidance that does not include compute revenue from China, and that sounds great, but what does it really mean? Anyway, regardless, it's going to be several quarters before we know how much Nvidia's business model will be negatively impacted. Until then, commentators can say pretty much anything they want. For me, the two major long-term risks for Anthropic are: one, LLM creators like Anthropic, lack moats, business moats. It's just too easy to switch between models, and that is why in October, Anthropic cut prices by 50%. It is seeking to stave off market share losses to the cheaper open weight models. And second, Anthropic has made 900 billion in various kinds of commitments. This means it will be raising debt at what I am sure will be expensive prices. It will be interesting to see what credit ratings the ratings agencies provide to Anthropic. A junk rating is not impossible. And three, Anthropic and OpenAI have to continue to evolve their product. They build frontier models and staying on the frontier is expensive. Anthropic is a business with slowing revenue growth, increasing expenses and increasing cost of capital. I just think there are too many risks here and that's why I am uninvolved. And onto the mailbag. There are two. First question, how do you position investments in a bear market versus a correction? A correction to me would be a decline in the stock market for a year or two. A true bear market lasts years. We have not had a bear market since the 1970s. I don't foresee a long-term bear market because the US economy is more dynamic than perhaps anytime in its history, and it is more dynamic I think than any other country in the world. Also, the banks are well capitalized. So even if the banks experience losses, they have the capital to withstand them. So how do you position for a correction? For those of you who are long-term investors, I would not do much of anything. I would continue to gradually buy the S&P 500 index, dollar cost averaging, and just not watch the market. The US will recover. But for those of you who are more trading oriented and can stomach paying capital gains taxes, I would sell some stocks and buy some combination of a healthcare index, a staples index and some short-term treasuries. The second question is from Jacob, who asks, Steve, I'm curious. How did you identify the liar loans if the information in them was falsified? Great question. First, some definitions. Pre-crisis, the subprime mortgage industry created a new type of loan. They called it a no doc or low doc loan. That means that very little underwriting was done. The buyer was simply asked what his income was, and the underwriter did not check it. They took the borrower at his or her word. Moody's securitization database provided information about these loans in each securitization. In examining the database, it became clear that starting in 2006, about 50% of all securitized subprime loans were low doc loans. We guessed about the amount that were liar loans and to this day, no one really knows the percentage of liar loans in the low doc loans. Jacob refers to liar loans, which are a large subset of the low doc loans. We just don't know how big. What's a liar loan? Some commentators have argued that the consumer simply lied about his or her income to get a mortgage loan. They blame the consumer. I don't think that that is what really happened. Instead, the underwriter would say to the borrower that to get this loan, they needed an income of, let's say, $50,000 a year. And the underwriter would then ask the borrower the level of his income. And at first, the borrower would be honest and say $40,000. The underwriter would then cajole the borrower to say $50,000, and finally, most did. That is what liar loans were all about. We guessed about the amount that were liar loans, but to this day, again, no one really knows the percentage of liar loans in the low doc loans. We see that in Korea a few weeks ago in terms of chips, but then the spending, I think investors have said, "We have to slow this down," not realizing that it was going to take a blue tsunami to actually slow it down. The interview was conducted just after the midterms, and we delved into the midterm implications as well as all aspects of the AI trade. Learn 90% of charisma in under 60 minutes

My First Million(974.0K)·57:53·18.0K views·2026-11-20

TL;DRCharisma is a learnable skill—not an innate trait—rooted in balancing warmth and competence (which together drive 82% of first impressions) to trigger oxytocin and dopamine in others.
By using purposeful body language, vocal confidence, playful conversational hooks, and strategic mirroring, leaders and professionals can build immediate trust and influence without faking authenticity. Warmth as a Lubricant for Competence: Vanessa Van Edwards notes that warmth...

TL;DR

Charisma is a learnable skill—not an innate trait—rooted in balancing warmth and competence (which together drive 82% of first impressions) to trigger oxytocin and dopamine in others. By using purposeful body language, vocal confidence, playful conversational hooks, and strategic mirroring, leaders and professionals can build immediate trust and influence without faking authenticity.

The Science of Charisma: Warmth, Competence, and Brain Chemistry

  • Warmth as a Lubricant for Competence: Vanessa Van Edwards notes that warmth and competence make up 82% of our impressions of people. Smart professionals often over-index on competence, which backfires because competence without warmth breeds suspicion.
    • Warmth is not "bubbly extroversion" or fake smiling; it shows up as vulnerability, humor, sarcasm, or even stumbling with words (e.g., Elon Musk’s unpolished vulnerability or Mark Zuckerberg’s everyday-guy demeanor).
  • Rejecting "Just Be Yourself": Both Sam Parr and Vanessa agree that "authenticity" and "just be yourself" are unhelpful advice for people trying to improve. Instead, charisma is about honing your own distinct flavor of communication.
  • The First-Minute Rule: First impressions are nearly irreversible; your first minute matters more than your first 30 minutes because the primitive brain is assessing friend vs. foe.
    • Oxytocin (Connection): Triggered in the first 3–5 seconds of eye contact, visible open hands (signaling no hidden weapons), and physical touch. A handshake produces roughly 3 hours' worth of eye-contact oxytocin.
    • Dopamine (Motivation & Energy): Triggered verbally by breaking social scripts and creating "information gaps" (e.g., Sam opening the show with a magic trick his daughter taught him, or Howard Stern asking, "Want to hear the weirdest thing that ever happened to me?"). Vanessa advises using information hooks every 1–2 minutes when speaking.

Mastering Conversation: Hooks, Small Talk, and Playfulness

  • Entertainers vs. Appreciators: Vanessa challenges Dale Carnegie’s advice to just ask lots of follow-up questions. While *Entertainers* love being the center of attention and answering questions, most VIPs are *Appreciators* who spend all day talking and want to be entertained or hear your stories.
    • Sam shares a story of dining with a ~$30 billion AI pioneer who spent the entire dinner asking Sam about his basic AI use cases because he was tired of being interviewed himself.
  • Dr. Dan McAdams’s Three Levels of Connection:
    • Level 1 (General Traits): Basic facts ("What do you do?"). Vanessa recommends swapping this for, *"Working on anything exciting these days?"*—a question she honed over 20 years.
    • Level 2 (Personal Concerns): Motivations, values, and goals. Transition here quickly by asking about goals tied to time boxes (quarters, New Year's, birthdays) or what someone is learning (e.g., books).
    • Level 3 (Self-Narrative): The story someone tells themselves about themselves.
  • Playfulness and Trigger Words: Keep a mental list of 20–50 "trigger words" (topics you enjoy) to steer conversations. Halting boring small talk with playful guessing games (e.g., guessing where someone is going on vacation or joking about baby names) triggers dopamine for both parties without requiring loud extroversion.
  • Being "Chalant": Sam and Vanessa emphasize that enthusiasm—the physical display of passion—is contagious. Citing a dating study by Monica Moore, Vanessa notes that availability signals make people more attractive than physical looks alone, both romantically and professionally.

Vocal Confidence, Language, and Leadership

  • Contagious Emotions & "Calm Assertive" Leadership: Vanessa cites an fMRI study where participants smelled sweat from skydivers and "caught" their fear in their amygdala. Sam connects this to his experience at age 21 training an 80-pound pit bull ("Jake's dog"), where he learned that leaders must be *calm assertive* rather than high-energy reactive.
  • Eliminating Uptalk: Using an accidental question inflection at the end of statements (e.g., stating a $10,000 price like a question) signals nervousness and deception, switching the listener's brain from listening to scrutinizing.
    • Men often do this in the first 10 seconds of a call/meeting while trying to sound warm; women often do it during the most nerve-wracking parts of a pitch.
  • Maximum Resonance Point: Every voice has a 10-to-12-note range. Speaking on the out-breath at the lower, relaxed end of your range projects confidence—without overcorrecting to an unnatural extreme.
  • Purposeful Language (Behavior Cues): In a prisoner’s dilemma study, groups told they were playing the "Wall Street game" shared an average of 1/3 of their profits, while groups told they were playing the "Community game" shared 2/3. Leaders should audit their sent emails and intentionally use achievement words (*win, succeed, master*) for performance days and collaborative words for brainstorming days.

Reading People, Hiring, and Mirroring VIPs

  • Hiring "Ambitious Heroes": Vanessa looks for candidates whose self-narrative is overcoming hurdles through grit.
    • Behavioral & Hypothetical Questions: Ask about the *most recent* hurdle they overcame (so it’s unrehearsed) or give them a real-time logistical hypothetical.
    • The Luck Test: Sam asks candidates, *"Do you feel lucky?"* Vanessa confirms research shows people who feel unlucky feel out of control and literally spot fewer opportunities.
    • The Character Question: From 10 years of research (Question #7 of 9 in her book), Vanessa asks: *"What book, movie, or TV character is most like you?"*—and warns never to argue with their answer, as it reveals how they predictably operate.
  • Finding Your Flavor: Rather than mimicking one celebrity, watch the top 10–20 TED Talks to see which delivery style resonates with you.
  • Mirroring VIPs: To charm high-status individuals without seeming like a clown (and with good, non-manipulative intent), mirror them on three planes:
    • Non-verbally: Match their animation, gestures, and pause lengths (e.g., matching a "2-second pauser").
    • Verbally: Adopt their exact lexicon (e.g., saying "clients" instead of "customers" if they do).
    • Tonally: Match their volume and speaking speed.

Vanessa Van Edwards’s Business Model & Growth

  • Milestones & Scale: Vanessa started posting YouTube videos every week in 2007. Today, her company has 10 employees, generated 300 million views last year, and recently hit the *New York Times* bestseller list (a 20-year goal).
  • Revenue Streams:
    • Corporate Speaking & Consulting: Her largest revenue bucket.
    • Courses & Teaching: Flagship course *People School* (office hours and workshops for leaders), LinkedIn Learning, MasterClass, and teaching a full-credit course at Harvard.
    • Book Royalties & Social Sponsors.
    • Corporate Certification: Certifying internal HR and tech staff to act as in-house communication coaches.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Corporate Certification Growth: Vanessa predicts that certifying internal communication coaches inside companies will be the primary focus and driver of her business over the next 5 years.
  • The "CCO" Ambition: Vanessa’s long-term bet is that—especially as AI reshapes communication—companies and schools will adopt her curriculum and install a certified Chief Communication Officer (CCO) or Chief Conversation Officer in every organization.

So they do a study where they brought people into their lab, they split them up into two different groups. The first group was told, "Hello, today you're going to play the Wall Street game," and they played a prisoner's dilemma type of game. In the second group, they came into the same lab with the same researchers and they said, "Hello, today you are going to play the community game," and they played the same prisoner's dilemma game. And this is the trick: the trials were exactly the same except for that one word change. They wanted to know if telling people they were playing the Wall Street game versus the community game would change anything about how they played. Everyone who was told they were playing the Wall Street game shared an average of one-third of their profits. Everyone who was told they were playing the community game shared an average of two-thirds of their profits. It's a massive behavior change for one word change. Modernizing Market Infrastructure | Material Matters with SEC Chairman Paul Atkins

U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission(25.0K)·45:04·2026-11-20
Paul AtkinsChairman, U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission Jamie SelwayDirector of the Division of Trading and Markets, U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission
TL;DRSEC Chairman Paul Atkins and Division of Trading and Markets Director Jamie Selway outline the Commission's push to modernize U.S. market infrastructure through a five-year "Innovation Exemption" sandbox for tokenized securities, an inevitable transition to 24/7 trading, and major cost-cutting reforms to the Consolidated Audit Trail (CAT).
). They also highlight closer CFTC collaboration, a shift away from regulation-by-enforcement, and upcoming rulemakings on Treasury clearing, off-channel communications, and the dealer definition. Division Structure: Director Jamie Selway leads ~230 staff across 8 offices—5...

TL;DR: SEC Chairman Paul Atkins and Division of Trading and Markets Director Jamie Selway outline the Commission's push to modernize U.S. market infrastructure through a five-year "Innovation Exemption" sandbox for tokenized securities, an inevitable transition to 24/7 trading, and major cost-cutting reforms to the Consolidated Audit Trail (CAT). They also highlight closer CFTC collaboration, a shift away from regulation-by-enforcement, and upcoming rulemakings on Treasury clearing, off-channel communications, and the dealer definition.

Division of Trading and Markets: Scope & Engagement

  • Division Structure: Director Jamie Selway leads ~230 staff across 8 offices—5 policy offices and 3 support offices (legal review, administration, and analytics/research).
    • Key units include the Office of Clearance and Settlement (led by Jeff Mooney, overseeing DTCC) and the Office of Broker Dealer Finance (previously led by Mike Macchiaroli, overseeing the net capital rule, customer protection rule, and SIPC coordination).
  • Market Complexity: The division oversees 1934 Act registrants, including 18 equity exchanges, 20 options exchanges (the 20th launched recently), thousands of broker-dealers, transfer agents, alternative trading systems (ATSs), security-based swap dealers, security-based swap SEFs, and SROs like FINRA.
  • Industry Outreach: Under Chairman Atkins' mandate, the division holds 8 to 10 industry meetings per week and has hosted 5 roundtables over the past 1.5 years (including a mid-September roundtable on 24/7 trading).

Tokenization & The "Innovation Exemption" Sandbox

  • Cross-Act Crypto Strategy: The 1934 Act strategy complements Chairman Atkins' 1933 Act "Reg Crypto Assets" proposal and Brian Daley's 1940 Act crypto custody strategy. Earlier 34 Act steps include the wallet statement, a DTCC no-action letter for blockchain clearance/settlement, and a tokenization taxonomy with CorpFin distinguishing true tokenized securities from synthetics or swaps.
  • Innovation Exemption Mechanics: Provides 5-year exemptive relief (cabined by volume thresholds) for U.S. persons to operate Tokenized Securities Venues (TSVs) trading true tokenized NMS securities (not synthetics).
    • Exempts TSVs from traditional exchange obligations, including market data reporting, CAT reporting, and Rule 611 (the trade-through rule).
    • Requires a notice filing, public website disclosures (detailing chains used, operations, and checks and balances), and 30-day issuer notice/consent before launching.
  • "Innovation Without Arbitrage": Both Atkins and Selway emphasize bringing tokenized activity onshore rather than losing it to offshore synthetics, while ensuring traditional exchanges and ATSs can also modify their rulesets to trade on-chain without being disadvantaged.
  • SIPC & On-Chain Payments: Questions remain on how SIPC will treat tokenized assets and dollar-based stablecoins. The SEC has already approved money market mutual funds for on-chain payment and is working with bank regulators to enable tokenized bank deposits.

The Path to 23/5 and 24/7 Trading

  • Current Baseline: U.S. equity markets have operated 16 hours a day (4:00 a.m. to 8:00 p.m. ET) for ~20 years. Broker-sponsored ATSs began 24/5 trading a couple of years ago, growing to ~1% of market volume without public market data.
  • Near-Term Milestones: Exchange 24 X (which applied ~3 years ago) plans to go live with exchange trading and public market data on December 6, operating on a 23/5 schedule (closing 8:00–9:00 p.m. ET for system resets and corporate actions). Over the summer, DTCC began clearing and settling 23/5.
  • 24/7 Logistics: One ATS has already announced plans to move to 24/7 trading. Key operational hurdles include prefunding weekend activity (since banking systems are closed), systematizing corporate actions (splits, dividends, halts), and recalibrating issuer earnings releases away from "after-hours" to maximize liquidity.

CAT Restructuring & CFTC Collaboration

  • CAT Cost & Governance Overhaul: The Consolidated Audit Trail (CAT) has ~$2 billion in sunk build-out costs and previously carried a $250 million/year run rate. Under Chairman Atkins, SROs helped reduce the annual run rate to just under $150 million.
    • Following a concept release, the SEC plans to assume governance of CAT and fund it via Section 31 fees, establishing cost-conscious governance (such as advisory committees) and adapting audit trails for new assets like perpetual single-stock futures, event contracts, and tokenized securities.
  • Ending the SEC–CFTC Turf War: Working with CFTC Chairman Selig, the agencies are collaborating on shared registrants and overlapping exposures (e.g., S&P 500, KPI products).
    • Joint requests for comment cover portfolio margining, definitional boundaries, and swap/security-based swap reporting alignment. Three registrants have applied to trade KPI products as security options.

Traditional Rulemaking, Exams, & Enforcement Reform

  • Treasury Clearing Mandate: Led with Commissioner Uyeda (originating from the Clayton era), requiring clearing of cash treasuries year-round in 2026 and repo clearing by mid-year 2027 to lower the cost of capital.
  • Transfer Agent & Dealer Rules: A transfer agent rule proposal is out for comment (the first update in over 50 years). A proposed dealer rule is nearing release to clarify that a dealer must have a customer/client nexus, addressing prior administration court losses.
  • Exams & Off-Channel Communications: Chairman Atkins directed Exams to act "more like teachers, less like police," using teach-ins and field feedback to tune rules rather than immediate enforcement referrals. Trading & Markets and Investment Management are nearing a proposal on off-channel communications (text messages) after past enforcement fines lacked any underlying insider trading or tipping.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • 24 X Exchange Launch: Exchange 24 X will go live with both exchange trading and public market data on December 6. — *Jamie Selway*
  • 24/7 Trading & Market Data: "We view 24/7 as being inevitable. So we'll get to formalizing market data and allowing exchanges to move forward in that fashion probably late next year." — *Jamie Selway*
  • Permanent Tokenization Rules: Rule proposals to make the Innovation Exemption concepts more permanent will be developed "next year." — *Jamie Selway*
  • CAT Restructuring: "I'm personally hopeful that we can complete sort of this, this one project [CAT restructuring] under your tenure. I think we can." — *Jamie Selway*
  • Treasury Clearing Deadlines: Clearing of cash treasuries will be required year-round in 2026, and clearing of repo by mid-year 2027. — *Jamie Selway*

Notable Quotes

"The more tokenized activity that happens in this country, vis-a-vis a synthetic alternative offshore, that's going to be best for our investors here." — *Chairman Paul Atkins*
"There's a large opportunity cost of preventing innovation... We're sort of seeing in real time what people think of what we're enabling." — *Jamie Selway*
"We want the Exams folks to, you know, be more like teachers, less like police." — *Chairman Paul Atkins (paraphrased by Jamie Selway)*
"Whether it's 23/5 now or 24/7 eventually, this is an advantage that the country should be pressing to really increase our lead over the pack in terms of global capital markets." — *Jamie Selway*

Obituaries | Stratechery by Ben Thompson

Stratechery(19.2K)·24:09·4.3K views·2026-11-20
Ben ThompsonHost, Stratechery Jensen HuangFormer CEO, Nvidia
TL;DRJensen Huang’s November 12, 2026 resignation as CEO of Nvidia—nominally triggered by personal affairs—marks the end of an era of tech company independence, as the U.S. government now treats AI hardware makers like defense contractors and banks rather than commercial businesses.
Without Huang’s singular leadership, Nvidia’s structural moats, supply chain relationships, and debt-fueled "neocloud" ecosystem face severe vulnerability. The Resignation: Jensen Huang resigned as CEO of Nvidia on Thursday, November 12, 2026, ending a 33-year tenure that began...

TL;DR: Jensen Huang’s November 12, 2026 resignation as CEO of Nvidia—nominally triggered by personal affairs—marks the end of an era of tech company independence, as the U.S. government now treats AI hardware makers like defense contractors and banks rather than commercial businesses. Without Huang’s singular leadership, Nvidia’s structural moats, supply chain relationships, and debt-fueled "neocloud" ecosystem face severe vulnerability.

The Fall of Jensen Huang and Nvidia's "Exoskeleton"

  • The Resignation: Jensen Huang resigned as CEO of Nvidia on Thursday, November 12, 2026, ending a 33-year tenure that began when he co-founded the company on April 5, 1993.
  • Nominal vs. True Cause:
    • Nominally, Huang stepped down due to affairs with at least two Nvidia employees and Alice Huang, an executive at Singaporean data center firm MegaSpeed (who is alleged to be a Chinese spy).
    • Fundamentally, Huang’s exit was caused by his refusal to accept that Nvidia was now viewed by Washington as a defense contractor.
  • The "Exoskeleton" Structure: Nvidia was uniquely organized around one man, operating like a startup with very little middle management, 60 direct reports to Huang, and no succession plan. While this enabled rapid, aggressive bets, it left no internal checks on Huang's behavior or compliance.
  • The Asian Supply Chain: Huang acted as a "one-person coordination function" for the Asian supply chain, famously eating and drinking across Asia to convince reluctant suppliers to commit capacity to Nvidia’s yearly release cycle.

The Erosion of Nvidia's Four Moats

  • Historical Dominance: Nvidia’s rise was built on the 2001 GeForce 3 (programmable pixel shaders), the 2006 launch of CUDA (an API that almost killed the company before becoming its core platform), and the 2019 acquisition of Mellanox (enabling scale-out systems). Following ChatGPT’s 2022 launch, Nvidia commanded margins exceeding 70%.
  • Moat 1 — CUDA (Eroding):
    • Developer energy has moved up the stack to LLM APIs, making CUDA less relevant for most companies.
    • Anthropic proved leading LLMs can eliminate Nvidia dependency by learning to run on Amazon’s Trainium chips and Google’s TPUs.
    • LLMs themselves are so effective at writing software that any software-based moat will inevitably dry up.
  • Moat 2 — Networking & Systems (Challenged): While more durable than CUDA, Nvidia's systems approach is increasingly challenged by Google and Anthropic, and relies heavily on neoclouds that struggle with power access and political permission.
  • Moat 3 — Supply Chain Mastery (Tenuous): Huang bragged that he never signed a contract with TSMC over their three-decade partnership, relying instead on a handshake and mutual trust. Without Huang, that commitment is in question.
  • Moat 4 — Organizational Speed (Collapsed): With Huang gone, the single-founder exoskeleton that allowed Nvidia to outmaneuver rivals is poised to collapse.

The AI Bubble (2025–2027) and Nvidia's "Risky Business"

  • Shifting Funding Mechanisms: The AI infrastructure build-out progressed from hyperscaler free cash flow, to debt (second half of 2025), and now to equity (starting with Google in summer 2026).
  • The Neocloud Hedge: To avoid complete dependence on hyperscalers (who are building their own chips), Nvidia aggressively sold to "neoclouds" (independent GPU renters).
  • Circular Financing: Because neoclouds face expensive debt and increasingly worthless equity, Nvidia used its own cash flow and credit rating to backstop and fund its own customers.
  • The $1 Trillion Threshold: Industry-wide AI revenue of roughly $1 trillion needs to materialize sooner rather than later. If it falls short, hyperscalers will prioritize their own compute, and Nvidia’s neocloud hedge will turn into a millstone, flooding the market with distressed assets.

AI as "Normal Technology" vs. China Hawks and Doomers

  • The Policy Battle: Huang argued AI was "normal technology" with engineering-solvable safety issues, putting him at odds with both AI "doomers" (e.g., Anthropic) and "China hawks," who viewed him as public enemy number one.
  • The China Dilemma: China traditionally accounted for roughly 25% of Nvidia’s sales. When the Biden administration imposed chip controls in 2022, Nvidia immediately released the A800 (an A100 with a third of its interconnects disabled), establishing a pattern of abiding by the letter—never the spirit—of the law.
  • The "Great Firewall" Playbook: China is currently banning imports of Nvidia’s two-generations-behind H20 chips (after the Trump administration banned and then reversed the ban). By cutting off access to Nvidia, China is forcing domestic demand to use inferior local alternatives—the exact strategy used with the Great Firewall to build Chinese internet giants—which will eventually enable a competing Chinese semiconductor ecosystem.

The End of Tech Company Independence (1968–2026)

  • The Intel Precedent: Since Robert Noyce founded Intel in 1968 (backed by Arthur Rock), tech companies operated on the premise that the world was their customer, volume drove progress, and executives—not the government—defined their products.
  • The Banking Standard: In a *Financial Times* interview, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent demanded answers from Nvidia’s board and outside counsel within 10 days and called for a "shake-up" with an external CEO.
  • Compliance Over Ignorance: Bessent criticized Howard Lutnick’s Commerce Department for delaying adding MegaSpeed to the U.S. blacklist, but argued Nvidia should be held to the same standard as a bank: ignorance is not a defense, and companies must put "boots on the ground" to enforce the spirit of U.S. sanctions.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • The U.S. Chip Ecosystem: Because "doomers and hawks have won" and AI will not be treated as normal technology, "in the fullness of time, it is the U.S. chip ecosystem that will pay the ultimate price." — *Ben Thompson*
  • Chinese Alternatives: By forcing internal demand onto domestic chips, China's homegrown alternatives and supporting semiconductor ecosystem "will flood the world" in the long run. — *Ben Thompson*
  • Neoclouds & Nvidia's Exoskeleton: Absent Huang’s willingness to shore up the ecosystem, Nvidia's neocloud hedge "is liable to turn into a millstone," and Nvidia's founder-centric exoskeleton "is much more poised to collapse than you might expect." — *Ben Thompson*

Notable Quotes

"Huang’s exit, nominally because of affairs with a buyer who may or may not have been a Chinese spy, as well as at least two company employees, was ultimately because Nvidia was, by the powers that be, considered a defense contractor and Huang never adjusted." — *Ben Thompson*
"If Nvidia 'were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the Treasury's Office of the Comptroller of the Currency would be in there right now and a lot of the executives would be removed.'" — *Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent*
"When AI was deemed to be anything but normal, however, business as usual wasn't just over. It was a crime, and it cost Huang his job, and maybe more." — *Ben Thompson*
"It makes it even more imperative that we sprint in the opposite direction of China towards more freedom and a bet on inventing new industries, not more control and a focus on gatekeeping what we have." — *Ben Thompson*

Naturally, this has introduced new complexity in managing those models.
