Dearly beloved, we are gathered here today to get through this thing called life. Prince. Jensen Huang, CEO of Nvidia, 1993 to 2026. Jensen Huang resigned as CEO of Nvidia on Thursday, November 12, 2026. He had been CEO of the company ever since he founded it on April 5th, 1993. Nvidia started out as a graphics card company. Huang's exit, nominally because of affairs with a buyer who may or may not have been a Chinese spy, as well as at least two company employees, was ultimately because Nvidia was, by the powers that be, considered a defense contractor and Huang never adjusted. In this, Huang was the ultimate victim of his own success. Nvidia's first graphics card simply translated triangles into pixels. The 2001 GeForce 3 introduced a programmable pixel shader, which meant a video card's functionality was no longer predefined. The killer move, however, which almost killed the company, was the 2006 introduction of CUDA, which was an API for programming those shaders. For much of the next 16 years, Huang sold the gospel of CUDA, convinced it was the platform for a future of accelerated computing. Then, in 2022, came ChatGPT. ChatGPT made clear what GPUs were most useful for: large language models, and Nvidia made the best GPUs. More than that, Huang had realized that the future of accelerated computing was not just chips, but rather systems. That drove the purchase of Mellanox in 2019, which provided the technology for Nvidia to scale out from chips, setting it down the road to being the best option for the sort of large-scale training that makes large language models possible. For this, Nvidia charged very high prices, with margins exceeding 70%, because they could. They had the platform, the technology, and, over the past few years, as others sought to make their own chips, the supply chain. Huang's impact on the Asian supply chain in particular cannot be overstated. Burn Hobart, in his book, Boom, Bubbles and the End of Stagnation, notes that one of the benefits of bubbles is that they provide a coordination function, as the fear of missing out incentivizes multiple entities with interlocking dependencies to all commit to building at the same time. Huang was, in many respects, a one-person coordination function. Huang gave multiple keynotes a year, which he explained as how he communicated with the ecosystem broadly, and was famous for eating and drinking his way across Asia in particular, convincing reluctant suppliers that the AI boom was real, and locking up the lion share of their output for Nvidia and its stunning commitment to a yearly release cycle. Huang, who had toiled for decades in relative obscurity, known primarily to gamers, and only barely to the tech industry, became a rock star, swarmed wherever he went, always in his black leather jacket. He also, to his ultimate detriment, became a political figure. Nvidia had long had no presence in either Washington, DC or Beijing, and was blindsided by the Biden administration's imposition of chip controls in 2022. Nvidia's immediate reaction to those initial controls, which restricted exports based on interconnect speed, was to immediately release the A800 in China, which was an A100 with a third of its interconnects disabled. That set the tone for the conflict that I believe was the true cause of Huang's downfall. Huang strenuously disagreed with the various iterations of the chip ban, and Nvidia only bothered to do the bare minimum to abide by the letter of the law, never the spirit. That approach, in all likelihood, extended to Nvidia's oversight, or lack thereof, of Singaporean entities that were alleged to be fronts for chips for China. Some of these were legal. Chinese companies are allowed to access cloud computing facilities outside of China that house leading edge Nvidia chips, although chip control advocates are seeking to close that loophole. Some were not legal, and that chips were ultimately ferried to China. MegaSpeed, whose executive, Alice Huang, was the first person Huang was alleged to have an affair with, was officially in the former business and alleged to actually be in the latter. There are whispers that MegaSpeed's ultimate customer was not or not just Alibaba, but also the Chinese military. These are whispers, to be clear. It was the affairs that were enough, at least publicly, to lead to Huang's resignation. Nvidia, most valuable company in the world, 2024 to 2026. Nvidia's durability, at least out of their margins, was already an open question. Start with CUDA: when the market was software written for GPUs, a platform—including not just an API, but a huge set of libraries targeting every conceivable market for accelerated computing—was a nearly impenetrable moat. That is why LLMs like GPT were written using CUDA. LLMs, though, both brought Nvidia's incredible success and also seeded its greatest risks. The first is that the vast majority of developer energy moved up the stack, using the LLM's API as a platform; for most companies, CUDA didn't matter. The second is that Anthropic, which found funding from Amazon and Google, originally also ran through the Microsoft-OpenAI partnership, had to learn how to use Trainium chips from Amazon and TPUs from Google. This was difficult, but ultimately meant that the leading LLM did not have any Nvidia dependency at all. Anthropic runs on Nvidia chips because they learned how to run on anything. The third is the most devastating: LLMs are so effective at running software that any software-based moat will inevitably dry up. Nvidia's second moat, its networking and systems approach, is more durable, although challenged by Google and, by extension, Anthropic in particular. Nvidia's systems approach is also the easy answer for enterprises and government seeking to own their own hardware, and neoclouds seeking to build data centers that are independent of the hyperscalers. The latter market rents chips to both hyperscalers and frontier labs, as well as individual companies, but is challenged as obvious sources of power and political permission disappear. They also need money to afford Nvidia systems, which has increasingly come from Nvidia itself. Nvidia's third moat is its mastery of the supply chain, and this is the moat that suddenly looks much more tenuous. Huang has bragged about the fact that he has never signed a contract with TSMC over their three decades of partnership. It's a relationship built on a handshake and mutual trust with Huang specifically. This is, per the point above, a very Asian way of doing business, and Huang was the master of that. It's questionable whether or not that trust and willingness to commit will persist when the handshake is no longer there. Nvidia's most important moat, however, was the degree to which it was not a normal company. Huang organized Nvidia as an exoskeleton around one man, himself. There is very little management in Nvidia. Huang famously has 60 direct reports. For the same reason that there was no succession plan. Nvidia could move so quickly and so aggressively because the company still behaved as if it were a startup. Indeed, lots of established companies, including those still led by founders, claim to still be a startup, but only one, Nvidia, really was organized that way. That paid off with all those successful bets. It also created an environment where there were no checks on Huang. And, now that he is gone, the exoskeleton is much more poised to collapse than you might expect. The AI bubble, 2025 to 2027. The first part of the AI build-out was funded by free cash flow, particularly from the hyperscalers. Then, during the second half of last year, nearly everyone started turning to debt. Now, starting with Google this past summer, the primary funding mechanism is equity. In the long run, this should come full circle, and future build-outs will once again be funded by free cash flow, generated from the infrastructure that is being built now. The problem for Nvidia is that the companies most able to navigate this progression are the hyperscalers, which have the resources to challenge Nvidia's hold on the chip market. To that end, Nvidia has been very eager to ensure that it is selling not only to the hyperscalers, but to other would-be competitors, like the neoclouds. There will likely, at some point, be serious questions raised about exactly how Nvidia went about allocating compute in pursuit of this goal. The neoclouds, however, are at a meaningful disadvantage when it comes to funding. First, their debt is significantly more expensive, given that their only business is renting GPUs, as opposed to, say, running the most valuable advertising businesses in the world, or the world's largest e-commerce platform, or dominating enterprise software. Second, their equity is increasingly worthless, given the significant risks attached to that credit. If a neocloud can't pay, it's the equity holders that get wiped out first. This led to what I termed Nvidia's risky business. Nvidia increasingly used its cash flow and credit rating to backstop and fund the companies who were buying its chips. Bears called this circular financing; bulls called it a necessary bridge to a future where AI revenue paid down those companies' debt and drove massive upside. What is undoubtedly true is that this was the sort of risky business that is uniquely possible for a multi-trillion dollar company that is completely controlled by one man, who has made an entire career of pushing the limits and making risky bets—and seeing them pay off. It's also a series of bets that are unlikely to continue. The neoclouds should be very nervous about losing their patron, along with their creditors and equity holders. The other problem is that the revenue necessary to make all this build-out work is something on the order of a trillion dollars industry-wide, and it needs to materialize sooner rather than later. If it falls short, it's the hyperscalers who will prioritize their own compute first, and they will likely be seen as a safe haven for everyone committing to spending in that world. Nvidia's neocloud hedge, absent Huang's willingness to shore the ecosystem up, is liable to turn into a millstone with their assets flooding the market even as the supply chain ramp-up brings ever more supply to market. AI as normal technology and the US semiconductor industry, 1947 to 2047. Perhaps because he had been preaching the gospel of accelerated computing for so long, Huang was the leading figure pushing back against the doomer narrative surrounding AI. Yes, AI was a massive business opportunity, but it was still normal technology. The safety issues presented were engineering challenges to be solved. What required consideration was not just the next 5 years, but the next 50. It's a view that I am, in full disclosure, generally aligned with. Many others are not. On one hand, there are the aforementioned doomers, certain that fast takeoff is imminent, or as already started, as AI increasingly builds better AI in a recursive loop. The end state is an AI that is massively more capable than humans, and uncontrollable by them. The only hope is to ensure that AI is aligned with humanity, by which adherence to this viewpoint mean aligned with them and their view of the world. This contingent is, broadly speaking, represented by Anthropic. And, in this view, actively working to enable and accelerate uncontrollable open weights models is a threat to humanity. On the other hand, there are the China hawks, who view AI as a national security issue, and the key to US military dominance in a world where China owns the means of production of most militarily relevant hardware. In this world, giving China access to chips is effectively treason against the United States. For both of these groups, Huang was public enemy number one, because of his determination to do what was best for Nvidia's business, which was to sell chips to China. This made sense for Nvidia for multiple reasons. The first and most obvious reason is that it is good for a company to be able to sell to everyone, including the second-largest market in the world. China has traditionally made up about a quarter of Nvidia's sales, long before AI was even a thing. The second is that China is a large enough market, with sufficiently motivated and capable developers, that it can actually create a meaningful CUDA alternative. Furthermore, such an alternative would, in the long run, not be constrained to China, but rather compete with Nvidia all over the world. The third is that China's AI ecosystem, unconstrained as it is by such niceties as terms of service, distills frontier lab models and releases them as open weights models. These models, meanwhile, are tuned to run on Nvidia chips. These models give Nvidia leverage versus the frontier labs all over the world, including in the West. The fourth is that China is uniquely positioned to create a fundamentally different approach to systems design using optical interconnects to scale out training clusters. This is much more inefficient in terms of power usage than Nvidia's more elegant designs, but China is also much more capable of manufacturing excess amounts of power to make it work. The fifth is that, in the very long run, China can create an entire alternative supply chain for semiconductors broadly, rendering Nvidia's mastery of the Western-aligned supply chain much less important. This last point is worth dwelling on. The chief objection that China hawks in particular make to the idea that it is a good idea to sell China chips, in order to prevent them from surpassing the US, is that China is already heavily invested in building their own ecosystem. This is certainly true, but I think it underplays the complexity of chips and what is necessary to actually compete with the West. Specifically, the Chinese government can only control the supply side, by spending money and making top-down imperatives. Building an ecosystem, however, whether that be for AI specifically or semiconductors broadly, requires demand as well. Progress in models and systems and chips depends on a feedback loop between the user and the developer, or the customer and the manufacturer. The demand side, however, is much more short-term oriented than the Chinese government might prefer. The model companies' concern is the other model companies: that means if they are distilling, we must distill too. If they are using Nvidia chips, then we must too. This matters up and down the stack, just as it has always mattered in technology, which really is different in this regard from almost every other industry. A useful analogy is to consider the internet. Why is it that country after country can profess a desire to have an indigenous alternative to the American tech giants, yet miserably fail in every attempt to generate it? It's because the tech giants are only a click away. In this regard, China's great firewall was one of the most brilliant geostrategic moves ever. By virtue of cutting off access to the American tech companies, China created the demand that pulled China's own tech giants into existence. You need demand, not just desire. This is why China itself is currently banning the import of Nvidia's two-generations-behind H20 chips, after the Trump administration foolishly banned their import, only to reverse themselves while bragging about the reason to do so. I keep China addicted to Nvidia. China is replaying the great firewall playbook: force its internal demand to use its much worse alternatives, because that is the only way those alternatives become better. And, in the long run, those alternatives, and the entire ecosystem that supports them, will flood the world. Huang was, to be perfectly clear, talking his book when it came to his insistence on selling chips to China, or in the case of the current controversy, allegedly turning a blind eye towards nominally legal buyers who were intent on violating the chip ban. That doesn't mean, however, that he was wrong about the long-term effects, at least if AI is a normal technology. And I haven't gotten to the risk this imposes on the Taiwan Strait. What is clear now, however, is that the doomers and hawks have won. AI, at least in terms of policy, will be treated as anything but normal, and in the fullness of time, it is the U.S. chip ecosystem that will pay the ultimate price. Tech company independence, 1968 to 2026. The birth date for the last obituary was the invention of the transistor. The birth date for this one is the founding of Intel. Robert Noyce and co-founders had spent the last decade at Fairchild Semiconductor, but were constrained by their parent company's fundamental conservatism and chafed at their lack of upside. Arthur Rock effectively created the venture capital industry in Silicon Valley through his financing of Intel. Noyce created the concept of the commercial market-led technology company that made it up in volume. At that time, the largest single purchaser of chips was the US government. Noyce realized that the fastest way to progress was to make chips for everyone, including the government, and on his terms, not theirs. From that point on, tech as an industry operated with the assumption that the world was its customer, and that their own executives defined the route to progress. Government was free to buy their products, but not to define them. If they wanted the best products, then they best not restrict who they could sell them to, either. That's how important volume was. And over time, it became clear how powerful and essential ecosystems were. Other industries operate differently. I thought this article in the Financial Times was striking, and not just because of the headline: Bessent says Nvidia needs 'shake-up' with external CEO. Bessent said that it is important, quote, "to examine whether Nvidia had a culture of compliance around the US export restrictions," and quote, "and whether its top executives knew they were being skirted." Quote, "Was it a low-level fail? Was it in the middle? Or was it up in the C-suite? And who knew? These shell companies were obvious. They were being investigated. And did Nvidia use them as a workaround to sell chips to the Chinese?" and quote, he said, quote, "Were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" and quote. The Treasury Secretary added that Nvidia needs to provide answers within a matter of days. Quote, "I think we need answers in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside council," and quote. He added that, quote, "We will see if Nvidia intentionally ignored the warning signs of these shell companies, many of them in Singapore." and quote. Bessent has pointed the spotlight on Howard Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to ban exports of Nvidia's chips to MegaSpeed, the Singaporean company linked to the alleged Chinese spy who had an affair with Huang. US authorities have been investigating whether MegaSpeed, which builds AI data centers in Asia, has been involved in smuggling high-end Nvidia chips, which are powering the AI boom, into China. Bessent has blamed the Commerce Department more than once this week for failing to ban exports of Nvidia's chips to MegaSpeed. Commerce delayed efforts to add MegaSpeed to Washington's blacklist of companies the US considers a risk to national security. Quote, "Many other departments were pushing for this, and Commerce had the pen," and quote, Bessent repeated on Sunday. Quote, "We have all the rules we need. We just need them enforced," and quote. Lutnick is under mounting pressure from within the administration and from Congress, which has demanded that he explain how he allowed chips to be exported to a company linked to an alleged Chinese spy. US lawmakers are also accelerating efforts to crack down on advanced AI chips reaching China. If Nvidia, quote, "were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the Treasury's Office of the Comptroller of the Currency would be in there right now and a lot of the executives would be removed," and quote, Bessent said. Scott Bessent is the Treasury Secretary, which is to say that he operates an apparatus that is arguably just as, if not more, powerful than the US military. Treasury controls the flow of money around the world, and its soldiers are the banks. If Treasury has sanctioned an entity, then it is up to banks to ensure those sanctions are enforced. They need to know their customers, and they need to analyze every dollar flowing through their systems. If they do not, they are breaking the law. Ignorance is not a defense. Ignorance, on the other hand, has been tech's defense basically forever. Yes, there are bad people in the world, which means that if the whole world is using your products, they will be used to do bad things. That was considered an acceptable trade-off, however, because most people are good and the benefits that come from scale, and an avoidance of the friction that comes from trying to track the activities of billions of people, were worth the risk. This was certainly what Huang believed, and to his detriment, he aggressively belittled anyone who dared disagree. The extent to which Huang is despised in Washington, DC, as evidenced by the giddiness and bloodlust online last week, is extraordinary. In Huang's view, as long as the Commerce Department said that it was okay to sell to MegaSpeed, then it was okay to sell to MegaSpeed and sleep with one of its executives. Bessent views the matter entirely differently. He thinks that Nvidia should operate like a bank. It's not just their job to abide by the letter of the law, but the spirit of it. It's not the government's job to enforce the chip ban, but the companies'. An attestation from a Singaporean customer that chips aren't going to China isn't worth anything. Actually putting boots on the ground and ensuring compliance is the only way Nvidia cannot be in violation of the US government's dictates. Again, for tech, this is extraordinary, even if it is normal in banking. Extraordinary enough, in fact, that Huang is being labeled by many, not just in Washington, but also in the finance world, as a traitor. Huang, in my estimation, is just a businessman who cares about nothing else other than Nvidia. That worked out great when he made GPUs for gaming that no one in government cared about or understood. That's just a normal technology. When AI was deemed to be anything but normal, however, business as usual wasn't just over. It was a crime, and it cost Huang his job, and maybe more. It's worth pointing out that while this episode was shocking, I should not, in retrospect, have been surprised. Earlier this year, when Anthropic faced off against the US government, I wrote in Anthropic and Alignment, quote, "At the same time, what is the standard by which it should be decided what is allowed and not allowed, if not laws, which are passed by an elected Congress? Anthropic's position is that Amodei, who I am using as a stand-in for Anthropic's management and its board, ought to decide what its models are used for, despite the fact that Amodei is not elected and not accountable to the public." And, on the second point, who decides when and in what way American military capabilities are used? That is the responsibility of the Department of War, which ultimately answers to the President, who also is elected. Once again, however, Anthropic's position is that an unaccountable Amodei may unilaterally restrict what its models are used for, and quote. My point in that article was that if AI is as exceptional as claimed, then the government will demand to say in how it is used, whether or not private company executives agree. Moreover, that was a good thing, or at least the least bad thing. The same analysis applies to Nvidia and Huang. Huang may have fundamentally disagreed with the chip ban, but at the end of the day, it was his duty to accept that it was the law of the land. To the extent he did not do so was ultimately his responsibility. American exceptionalism, 1776 to 2026. Perhaps the most tragic aspect of the Huang affair is the extent to which he was the personification of the American dream. Huang was sent to America as a nine-year-old who didn't speak a lick of English, and somehow ended up at a boarding school in rural Kentucky for misbehaving youth, where he would trade help on homework for protection from bullies. From there he went to a state school in Oregon, became an engineer, before famously ending up at a Denny's diner where he and his co-founders decided to start Nvidia. Then, over the following 33 years, he took risk after risk, created innovation after innovation, until he became a hero not just in the United States, but across the world. And then, as you might say, he flew too close to the sun. Certainly, he did so in his private life. It's not just the affairs, which may be par for the course for rich and powerful men. Rather, it's notable just how reckless the affairs were. The ones that were revealed last week were with a customer and potential Chinese spy, and two of his own employees. Both are fireable offenses. Meanwhile, the way in which Huang treated his opponents, including actively seeking to get people fired who he felt stood in the way of getting his way, bore the marks of a man who has constructed a life and a company where no one can or will stand up to him. That is a recipe for disaster, and here we are. At the same time, it really does bum me out that what ultimately doomed Huang was a set of rules that really are a big departure from the status quo, and in my opinion, misguided. Huang's actions or lack of actions in terms of enforcing compliance with the chip ban are treated as if they are an obvious crime, when in fact this is a dramatic shift in expectations from a short time ago. Although there is certainly a callback to previous controversies around selling advanced computing to adversaries. Huang should have adjusted and accepted the new reality, make no mistake. I'm sympathetic to the fact he couldn't, and recognize the failure in not having structures in place that could have warned him about the dangerous road he was embarking on. Even as I realize Huang's willingness to navigate dangerous roads is precisely why he was an American success story. Beyond Huang, however, there is the question of how we compete with China, and I'm disappointed, although not surprised, that we are pursuing the route of bans and forced compliance instead of innovation and openness. Make no mistake, I'm no China dove, and I've been very early to the argument that we erred greatly in willingly exporting entire industries to the communist state. That, however, makes it even more imperative that we sprint in the opposite direction of China towards more freedom and a bet on inventing new industries, not more control and a focus on gatekeeping what we have. I get the logic, particularly in the case of AI, which is extraordinary. I lament the implications of what it says about our belief in ourselves and our ability to stay ahead. This article was presented by Stripe. Even as AI models become more capable, you might expect the market will consolidate around a handful of providers, but user data tells a different story. Among OpenRouter customers spending at least $100 a month, the median number of AI model families in use has doubled over the past year from three to six. Even as AI models become more capable, businesses are using more models, not fewer. This makes sense when you consider that intelligence isn't one-dimensional. A model that's exceptional at writing code may not be the fastest at answering customer questions, or the most economical in processing millions of documents. The question is shifting from "which AI model should we build on" to "which model should handle each task." The answer, increasingly, is "it depends," which is great for flexibility, less great for simplicity. President Trump Hosts a News Conference

The White House(3.4M)·88:18·350.9K views·2026-11-20

TL;DRPresident Donald Trump, joined by Vice President JD Vance, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, and Chief of Staff Stephen Miller, recapped a four-day Asia trip highlighted by a historic US–Russia–China trilateral nuclear summit, over $17 billion in ASEAN commercial deals, and new trade agreements with Thailand and the Philippines.
Domestically, the administration touted Senate-passed permitting reform, 10 straight months of manufacturing growth, and a 97% drop in maritime fentanyl, while announcing a ban on US companies building data centers abroad and a $50 billion request to overhaul air traffic...

TL;DR

President Donald Trump, joined by Vice President JD Vance, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, and Chief of Staff Stephen Miller, recapped a four-day Asia trip highlighted by a historic US–Russia–China trilateral nuclear summit, over $17 billion in ASEAN commercial deals, and new trade agreements with Thailand and the Philippines. Domestically, the administration touted Senate-passed permitting reform, 10 straight months of manufacturing growth, and a 97% drop in maritime fentanyl, while announcing a ban on US companies building data centers abroad and a $50 billion request to overhaul air traffic control.

Asia Trip, Trade Deals & Global Diplomacy

  • Trip Overview & Air Force One: President Trump reported meeting more than 20 world leaders, attending four major summits, securing two new trade deals (with ~10 more pending), and completing a record 14.5-hour nonstop Air Force One flight (4 hours shorter than the previous record, compared to a typical 20-hour flight with fuel stops).
  • First-Ever US–Russia–China Trilateral Summit: Held in Shenzhen, China, bringing together the nations holding 92% of the world's nuclear weapons.
      • Nuclear & Security Agreements: The three nations signed a trilateral peace declaration stating nuclear conflict must never happen. China agreed for the first time to trilateral nuclear reduction talks (Trump noted the US has ~4,000 nuclear weapons, Russia ~3,000+, and China ~600).
      • Energy & ICC: The three leaders agreed to increase oil, gasoline, and diesel production, and jointly condemned the International Criminal Court (ICC).
  • ASEAN & Bilateral Deals (Manila): President Trump secured more than $17 billion in commercial deals across 14 ASEAN nations.
      • Philippines: Philippine Airlines will purchase 20 Boeing 787 Dreamliners with General Electric engines (supporting 47,000 US jobs). Following a state visit with President Ferdinand Marcos, the Philippines committed to buying >$2 billion in US military equipment, 100% of its agricultural imports (starting January 1), and 100% of its energy (up from 20%) from the US, alongside a framework deal slashing Philippine tariffs.
      • Thailand: Signed an agreement eliminating all tariffs and non-tariff barriers on US products and committing to buy $2.6 billion/year in US agricultural goods.
      • Cloud Infrastructure: Both the Philippines and Malaysia agreed to use US technology for their cloud infrastructure.
  • Quad Leaders Summit (Manila): Leaders of the US, India, Australia, and Japan launched the first-ever Quad Bureau Leaders Forum (for economic security and supply chain resiliency), a Quad Super Intelligence (SI) accelerator, and new maritime security and surveillance collaboration.
  • APEC & Western Hemisphere (Shenzhen):
      • China: Trump noted fentanyl entering the US by sea is down 97%, citing his prior 145% tariffs and 20% fentanyl penalty.
      • Mexico & Canada: Trump discussed cartels and trade surpluses with Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum, and pushed Canadian PM Carney on the Keystone Pipeline and the $80 billion US trade deficit with Canada.
  • Ukraine, Iran & Greenland:
      • Ukraine–Russia: Trump met with President Putin, noting 25,000–30,000 soldiers (averaging 25,933/month) are dying. He criticized the Biden administration for giving Ukraine $350 billion in cash and equipment, noting the US now sells weapons to the EU.
      • Iran: Trump stated Iran "cannot be allowed to have a nuclear weapon," noting the US Navy has total control over the Strait of Hormuz and that Iran has killed 75,000 protesters over the last eight months.
      • Greenland: Trump announced Greenland was "just approved yesterday," thanking Denmark and NATO Secretary General Mark Rutte.

Economic Performance, Manufacturing & Tech Policy

  • Macroeconomic Metrics: Trump reported 10 straight months of manufacturing growth, a 21% increase in new equipment investment this year, $25 trillion in new wealth created since taking office (with $4 trillion lost since the midterms), and an all-time record low poverty rate.
  • Energy Prices: Crude oil is down 20% since early November to ~$80/barrel, and gasoline is down 60 cents/gallon in the last month (hitting $1.85/gallon in Iowa prior to the Iran operation).
  • Federal Reserve & Interest Rates: Trump criticized the "political Fed" for raising rates, stating each percentage point costs $650 billion and arguing lower rates could push US GDP to 14–20% (compared to India's 7%).
  • Manufacturing & Defense Reshoring: Audi announced a $4 billion plant, Toyota is building a $5 billion plant, and GM and Ford are expanding—including a 1906 Detroit Ford plant now operating 24/7. Defense contractors are also building 21 new plants for Patriots, Tomahawks, and THAAD systems.
  • Super Intelligence (SI) & Data Centers:
      • Trump and President Xi agreed to establish joint guardrails on SI.
      • Trump announced the US will not allow American companies to build data centers abroad (e.g., in Finland), threatening "tremendous" taxes and penalties. He cited local deals where data centers pay teachers $50,000 in Louisiana and residents $10,000 apiece elsewhere.
      • Trump praised Anthropic's recent launch and noted the US is 10 years ahead of China in chipmaking, stating only older chips—not Nvidia's latest Blackwell chip—should go to China.

Domestic Policy, Infrastructure & Bipartisan Prospects

  • Permitting Reform: Landmark permitting reform legislation passed the Senate and heads to the House, projected to put at least $1,300/year back into Americans' pockets.
  • Air Traffic Control (ATC) & Tech Outages: Trump attributed recent bank and corporate outages to separate, routine system upgrades. However, he called the 55-year-old US ATC system "obsolete" and requested $50 billion from Congress (on top of $12 billion already allocated) to build a unified, modern system.
  • Crime & Border Security: Trump reported D.C. crime is down 87%, Memphis crime is down 81%, and the US murder rate is at a 125-year low (since 1900). He stated zero people have entered the US illegally in the last 18 months (compared to 25–30 million under Biden).
  • Bipartisan Legislation: Trump expressed openness to working with Democratic leaders Hakeem Jeffries and Chuck Schumer on healthcare, a federal entertainment/film production incentive bill (backed by Sen. Adam Schiff), and a college sports/NIL bill (backed by Randy Levine and Coach Nick Saban) to prevent colleges—some losing $500 million/year—from going bankrupt.

Administration Perspectives (Vance, Rubio, Miller)

  • Vice President JD Vance: Connected the administration's global trade deals with the Senate's bipartisan permitting reform, arguing both are required to incentivize domestic investment in US workers and factories. On the Iran operation, Vance defended the goal of preventing a nuclear Iran, stating his job is to support the President's decisions.
  • Secretary of State Marco Rubio: Explained that ASEAN, the Quad, and APEC directly create markets and jobs for American workers. He emphasized that regular engagement among the US, Russia, and China—the world's three largest nuclear powers—is essential for global safety and sets the tone for diplomacy.
  • Chief of Staff Stephen Miller: Highlighted the administration's record, including 10-to-1 net deregulation, ending DEI, record fossil fuel and coal production, the first new nuclear reactors since the 1970s, taking over Venezuela in under an hour (doubling US oil reserves), and defeating 20%+ inflation.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Pending Trade Deals: About 10 additional trade deals are "coming very soon" — *President Trump*.
  • Permitting Reform Impact: If passed by the House, permitting reform is projected to put at least $1,300 a year back in the pockets of hardworking Americans — *President Trump*.
  • China's Nuclear Arsenal: China is currently behind on nuclear weapons but "will catch us within 10 years" — *President Trump*.
  • Chip Obsolescence: "In two years, Blackwell will be obsolete" — *President Trump*.
  • Quad Business Forum: Top businesses from the US, India, Australia, and Japan will meet "early next year" under the Quad Bureau Leaders Forum — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*.
  • Administration Trajectory: "The next two years are going to be the greatest two years in American history" — *Chief of Staff Stephen Miller*.

President Trump News Conference

C-SPAN(2.0M)·89:10·109.3K views·2026-11-20
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Xi JinpingPresident, China Vladimir PutinPresident, Russia
TL;DRFollowing a four-day trip to Asia that culminated in the first-ever trilateral summit between the United States, Russia, and China, President Donald Trump touted a series of major trade deals, a trilateral nuclear risk-reduction declaration, and domestic permitting reform, while facing ongoing domestic debate over the Iran conflict and recent tech outages.
First-Ever Trilateral Summit: President Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese President Xi Jinping held a historic trilateral meeting in Shenzhen, China, signing a first-of-its-kind peace declaration stating that nuclear conflict must never occur. Nuclear...

TL;DR

Following a four-day trip to Asia that culminated in the first-ever trilateral summit between the United States, Russia, and China, President Donald Trump touted a series of major trade deals, a trilateral nuclear risk-reduction declaration, and domestic permitting reform, while facing ongoing domestic debate over the Iran conflict and recent tech outages.

Historic Asia Trip & US-Russia-China Trilateral Summit

  • First-Ever Trilateral Summit: President Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese President Xi Jinping held a historic trilateral meeting in Shenzhen, China, signing a first-of-its-kind peace declaration stating that nuclear conflict must never occur.
    • Nuclear Balance: Trump noted the three nations hold 92% of the world's nuclear power, estimating the U.S. has 4,000 nuclear weapons and China has 600. China agreed for the first time to conduct trilateral discussions on reducing nuclear risk.
    • Energy & ICC: The three leaders agreed to work together to increase oil, gasoline, and diesel production, and China joined in condemning the International Criminal Court (ICC).
    • Diplomatic Rationale: Secretary of State Marco Rubio emphasized that regular engagement among the three largest nuclear powers is essential, arguing it sets a positive foundation for trade negotiations and global safety.
  • Skeptical Assessment of China-Russia Ties: Prior to the press conference, former National Security Adviser John Bolton warned that Xi and Putin have formed a "partnership without limits" where China is the dominant partner, creating a tri-polar nuclear world for which the U.S. lacks a strategy.
  • Ukraine & Bilateral Meetings: Trump stated that Putin expressed a desire to end the Russia-Ukraine war diplomatically, noting that 25,000 soldiers are being killed every month. Trump also met with Xi on economic progress, noting fentanyl coming into the U.S. by sea is down 97%.

Trade Deals, Tariffs & Economic Metrics

  • Southeast Asia & ASEAN Deals: At the ASEAN summit in Manila, Trump secured more than $17 billion in commercial deals, including:
    • Philippines: Philippine Airlines will purchase 20 Boeing 787 Dreamliners with General Electric engines (supporting 47,000 U.S. jobs). The Philippines also agreed to buy 100% of its agricultural products (starting January 1) and 100% of its energy from the U.S., alongside more than $2 billion in U.S. military equipment.
    • Thailand: Signed an agreement to eliminate all tariffs and non-tariff barriers on U.S. products and purchase $2.6 billion per year in U.S. agricultural goods.
  • North American Trade: Trump noted he is close to a deal with Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum on trade surpluses and cartels, while criticizing Canada over an $80 billion U.S. trade deficit.
  • Domestic Economic Claims: Trump highlighted 10 straight months of manufacturing growth, a 21% increase in new equipment investment, $25 trillion in new wealth created since taking office (though $4 trillion was lost after the election), and record-low poverty.
    • Auto Manufacturing: Trump cited new U.S. investments driven by tariffs, including a $4 billion Audi plant, a $5 billion Toyota plant, and the expansion of a Detroit Ford plant originally built in 1906.
    • Energy Prices: Crude oil has dropped 20% since early November to nearly $80 a barrel, and gasoline is down 60 cents a gallon from a month ago.

Super Intelligence (SI), Data Centers & Domestic Permitting

  • Global AI/SI Competition: Trump stated the U.S. leads China in "Super Intelligence" (SI) and agreed with Xi to establish joint guardrails. The U.S. also launched a Quad SI Accelerator with India, Australia, and Japan.
  • Ban on Overseas Data Centers: Trump announced that the U.S. will not allow American companies to build data centers in other countries (citing a proposed facility in Finland), threatening "tremendous" taxes and penalties to keep construction—and energy infrastructure—domestic.
    • He noted local communities are benefiting financially, citing deals in Louisiana paying teachers $50,000 and another area giving residents $10,000 apiece.
  • Permitting Reform: The Senate passed a bipartisan permitting reform bill that Vice President JD Vance said will enable unprecedented domestic construction of homes, factories, and power plants. Trump projected the bill will put at least $1,300 a year back into Americans' pockets.

Iran Conflict, Oil Blockade & War Powers Debate

  • Senate War Powers Vote: For the 15th time, Senate Republicans blocked a War Powers resolution to end hostilities in Iran, failing 48–51. GOP Senators Susan Collins, Lisa Murkowski, and Rand Paul joined most Democrats in favor, while Democrat John Fetterman and Republican Tom Tillis voted against.
    • Opposition View: Sen. Tim Kaine (D-VA) argued the war is unconstitutional and violates the 1973 War Powers Act's 60-day limit, calling the November 3 election results a repudiation of the conflict.
  • Administration Stance: Trump stated Iran "will never have a nuclear weapon, ever," and noted the U.S. Navy's blockade gives the U.S. total control over the Strait of Hormuz, allowing record amounts of oil to move out. Vice President Vance argued on Fox News that the U.S. has destroyed Iran's nuclear program and conventional military.
  • Bolton's Critique: John Bolton argued that while the U.S.-Israeli strikes and oil blockade have devastated Iran's economy and leadership, Trump lacks a clear, long-term strategy for regime change and failed to coordinate with NATO, Gulf Arab states, or Asian allies before the strikes began.

Domestic Issues & Tech Outages

  • System Outages: Addressing public concern over recent banking, credit card, and air traffic control outages, Trump said the incidents—such as Chase and other companies going down—are separate events caused by thousands of companies simultaneously upgrading their systems, not a coordinated crisis.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • China's Nuclear Catch-Up: "China is way behind, but they'll catch us within 10 years, they're going at it hot and heavy." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Permitting Reform Savings: The bipartisan permitting reform bill, if passed by the House, is "projected to put at least $1,300 a year back in the pockets of hardworking Americans." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Philippines Agricultural Purchases: The Philippines will "buy 100% of your agriculture from January 1st from our farmers." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Quad Business Forum: The top businesses from the U.S., India, Australia, and Japan "are going to meet early next year under the auspices of the President's sponsorship... and hopefully come up with additional commercial agreements." — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*
  • Iran Regime Change: "The most likely outcome in the near term would be that the existing regime disintegrates... and in the near term, you end up with some kind of military government, not led by the Revolutionary Guard, but by the regular military." — *John Bolton*

Notable Quotes

"These three countries, the United States, Russia and China, between these three countries possess the overwhelming majority of nuclear weapons on this planet. The idea that these three leaders should not engage on a regular basis is not just absurd, it's dangerous." — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*
"I think what's really going on here is that there's a Chinese-Russian axis emerging. It may look a lot like the Sino-Soviet alliance of the Cold War, but actually, in this incarnation, it's China that's dominant, China really calling the shots." — *John Bolton, Former National Security Adviser*
"More than anything, I think the President has done, and not just his second term, but his first term as well, is that he's reorganized the global economy so that it makes sense to invest in the United States of America again." — *Vice President JD Vance*

TL;DR

President Donald Trump, joined by Vice President JD Vance, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, and Chief of Staff Stephen Miller, returned from a four-day Asia trip highlighted by the first-ever trilateral summit with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin, $17 billion in new commercial deals, and major trade agreements with Thailand and the Philippines. Domestically, Trump touted $25 trillion in new wealth creation, outlined plans to tax U.S. companies building data centers abroad, requested $50 billion to overhaul the U.S. air traffic control system, and signaled openness to bipartisan deals on healthcare, immigration, entertainment, and college sports.

Historic Asia Trip, Trilateral Summit & Global Diplomacy

  • First-Ever US-Russia-China Trilateral Summit: In Shenzhen, China, Trump met jointly with President Xi Jinping and President Vladimir Putin, calling it the most momentous summit in years.
    • Nuclear Risk Reduction: The three nations—which hold 92% of the world's nuclear weapons (U.S. ~4,000, Russia ~3,000+, China ~600)—signed a formal trilateral peace declaration agreeing nuclear conflict must never happen and launching first-ever trilateral talks on nuclear risk reduction.
    • Energy & International Courts: The three leaders agreed to increase oil, gasoline, and diesel production to lower global prices and jointly condemned the International Criminal Court (ICC) for overreach.
    • Russia-Ukraine War: Trump held a bilateral meeting with Putin, stating Putin wants a diplomatic end to the war, which is killing an average of 25,933 soldiers per month. Trump noted deep personal hatred between Putin and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy remains the primary obstacle.
  • Quad & ASEAN Summits in Manila: Trump attended the Quad Leaders Summit (U.S., India, Australia, Japan) and addressed 14 nations at ASEAN.
    • Quad Initiatives: Announced the first-ever Quad Bureau Leaders Forum (meeting early next year), a new Quad Superintelligence (SI) accelerator, and expanded maritime security and surveillance cooperation.
    • Greenland & Undersea Defense: Trump announced that U.S. control/access to Greenland was approved the previous day, thanking Denmark and NATO's Mark Rutte, and highlighted new efforts on cybersecurity and defending undersea cables.
  • Record-Setting Flight: Trump noted the new Air Force One completed the return flight in 14.5 hours without refueling—beating the previous record by 4 hours and avoiding the typical 20-hour flight with fuel stops.
  • Rubio's Diplomatic Framing: Secretary of State Marco Rubio emphasized that regular engagement among the U.S., Russia, and China is essential, calling the idea of not engaging "absurd" and "dangerous" given their nuclear arsenals, and noting that head-of-state relationships set the tone for trade negotiators and diplomats.

Trade Deals, Tariffs & Domestic Manufacturing

  • Philippines & Thailand Trade Agreements: Trump secured two major trade deals during the trip, alongside 10 more expected soon.
    • Thailand: Agreed to eliminate all tariffs on U.S. products, remove non-tariff barriers, and purchase $2.6 billion annually in U.S. agricultural goods (which Trump plans to increase).
    • Philippines: Reached a framework deal to eliminate the U.S. trade deficit by slashing Philippine tariffs and committing the Philippines to buy 100% of its agricultural products (starting January 1) and 100% of its energy from the U.S.
    • Commercial & Defense Contracts: Secured over $17 billion in commercial deals at ASEAN, including Philippine Airlines purchasing 20 Boeing 787 Dreamliners with General Electric engines (supporting 47,000 U.S. jobs), cloud infrastructure agreements with the Philippines and Malaysia, and over $2 billion in U.S. military equipment purchases by the Philippines.
  • North American Trade:
    • Mexico: Met with President Claudia Sheinbaum; nearing a deal to address Mexico's trade surplus and cartel activity.
    • Canada: Met with Prime Minister Carney; Trump cited an $80 billion U.S. trade deficit with Canada and said talks will advance once it reaches zero, though he expressed support for Carney's desire to build the Keystone pipeline.
  • Domestic Economic & Manufacturing Metrics:
    • Wealth & Markets: Trump stated the U.S. has created $25 trillion in new wealth in under two years, though $4 trillion was lost following the recent midterm elections.
    • Industrial Growth: Reported 10 consecutive months of manufacturing growth (a record) and a 21% increase in new equipment investment this year.
    • Auto Reshoring: Cited thousands of factories under construction due to tariffs, including a $4 billion Audi plant, a $5 billion Toyota plant, expansions by General Motors and Ford, and the revival of Ford's 1906 Detroit plant—now running 24/7 instead of closing.
    • Energy Prices: Crude oil has dropped 20% since early November to nearly $80/barrel, and gasoline is down 60 cents per gallon over the past month (having hit $1.85/gallon in Iowa prior to U.S. military action against Iran).
    • Vance's Economic Summary: Vice President JD Vance credited Trump with reorganizing the global trade system to incentivize domestic investment, linking the overseas trade wins with the Senate's passage of bipartisan permitting reform legislation to build more homes, factories, and power plants.

Superintelligence (SI), Data Centers & Tech Policy

  • US-China SI Guardrails: Trump and Xi Jinping agreed to establish joint guardrails on "superintelligence" (SI), noting the U.S. leads and China is second with no close third place.
  • Blocking Foreign Data Centers: Trump announced that the U.S. will not allow American companies to build data centers overseas (specifically citing a planned facility in Finland), threatening "tremendous" taxes and penalties on companies that try, while encouraging domestic builds in states like Texas, California, and West Virginia.
    • Highlighted local community deals, such as data centers paying Louisiana teachers $50,000 for approvals or giving local residents $10,000 each, while building their own power plants to lower local electricity costs.
  • Permitting Reform: The Senate passed landmark permitting reform legislation projected to save Americans at least $1,300 per year and accelerate data center and plant construction.
  • Chips & Nvidia: Trump stated the U.S. is 10 years ahead of China in chipmaking and will only allow older chips—not Nvidia's latest Blackwell chip—to be sold to China. He also praised Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang as "brilliant" and suggested he be given a second chance and allowed to return to work after an apology.

Domestic Policy, Infrastructure, Crime & Bipartisan Outlook

  • Air Traffic Control & Bank Outages:
    • Air Traffic Control: Trump called the 55-year-old U.S. air traffic control system "obsolete" (requiring flights to move in "slow motion" to stay safe) and asked Congress for $50 billion—on top of $12 billion already allocated—to hire a single primary contractor (such as Raytheon or IBM) to rebuild the system and rehab/replace towers.
    • Corporate Outages: Attributed recent bank (e.g., Chase), travel, and credit card outages to separate, isolated company software upgrades rather than a coordinated event.
  • Iran & National Security:
    • Iran: Trump defended U.S. military action in Iran, stating the U.S. Navy has a blockade and "total control" over the Strait of Hormuz, extracting record oil last week. He vowed Iran—which he said killed 75,000 protesters over the past eight months—will never get a nuclear weapon and warned against any "dirty bomb" plot in Washington, D.C.
    • Vance on Midterms: Vice President Vance stated that preventing Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon is worth the political cost, noting his role is to support the President's decisions.
  • Crime & Border Security:
    • Crime Drops: Trump reported D.C. crime is down 87%, Memphis crime is down 81%, New Orleans had its safest Mardi Gras on record, and national murder rates are at a 125-year low (since 1900). He offered federal assistance to Chicago, Los Angeles, and New York.
    • Border: Claimed zero illegal border crossings over the past 18 months (compared to 25–30 million under Biden) and noted a productive call with the newly elected Governor of Texas.
  • Bipartisan Legislation & Stephen Miller's Assessment:
    • Congressional Outlook: Trump said he will use his veto power to negotiate with incoming Speaker Hakeem Jeffries and Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, eyeing bipartisan deals on healthcare, immigration, an entertainment/film production incentive bill (noting support from Adam Schiff), and a college sports/NIL bill (backed by Randy Levine and Nick Saban) to cap $12 million quarterback deals and prevent colleges from losing $500 million a year and going bankrupt.
    • Miller's Recap: Chief of Staff Stephen Miller praised Trump's first two years as the most successful in U.S. history, citing a 10:1 net deregulation ratio, ending DEI, record fossil fuel and coal production, new nuclear reactors going critical for the first time since the 1970s, taking over Venezuela in under an hour to double U.S. oil reserves, and defeating 20%+ inherited inflation.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Trade Deals: About 10 additional trade deals will be secured "very soon." — *President Donald Trump*
  • China Nuclear Parity: China will catch up to the U.S. in nuclear weapons capacity "within 10 years." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Chip Obsolescence: Nvidia's latest Blackwell chip will be obsolete "in two years." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Philippines Agriculture: The Philippines will buy 100% of its agricultural products from the U.S. starting "January 1st." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Permitting Reform Savings: The Senate-passed permitting reform bill is projected to put "at least $1,300 a year back in the pockets of hardworking Americans." — *President Donald Trump*
  • Quad Business Forum: Top businesses from the four Quad nations (U.S., India, Australia, Japan) will meet "early next year" to negotiate new commercial agreements. — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*
  • Next Two Years: "The next 2 years are going to be the greatest 2 years in American history." — *Chief of Staff Stephen Miller*

Notable Quotes

The idea that these three leaders should not engage on a regular basis is not just absurd, it's dangerous." — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*

Trump Threatens to Tax Firms Building Data Centers Abroad: Full Presser

Bloomberg Podcasts(627.0K)·87:39·17.6K views·2026-11-20
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Xi JinpingPresident, China Vladimir PutinPresident, Russia
TL;DRFollowing a four-day Asia trip that included a historic trilateral summit with China's President Xi Jinping and Russia's President Vladimir Putin, President Donald Trump announced plans to heavily tax U.S. companies that build data centers abroad, detailed over $17 billion in new Pacific trade deals, and pushed for a $50 billion overhaul of the U.S. air traffic control system.
First-Ever US-Russia-China Trilateral Summit: President Trump met with President Xi Jinping and President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China, focusing on nuclear de-escalation among the three nations, which hold 92% of the world's nuclear weapons (Trump noted the U.S. has ~4,000,...

TL;DR: Following a four-day Asia trip that included a historic trilateral summit with China's President Xi Jinping and Russia's President Vladimir Putin, President Donald Trump announced plans to heavily tax U.S. companies that build data centers abroad, detailed over $17 billion in new Pacific trade deals, and pushed for a $50 billion overhaul of the U.S. air traffic control system.

Asia Trip, Trilateral Summit & Global Security

  • First-Ever US-Russia-China Trilateral Summit: President Trump met with President Xi Jinping and President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China, focusing on nuclear de-escalation among the three nations, which hold 92% of the world's nuclear weapons (Trump noted the U.S. has ~4,000, Russia is second, and China has 600).
    • The three leaders signed a formal trilateral peace declaration stating nuclear conflict must never happen, with China agreeing for the first time to trilateral nuclear risk discussions and to condemn the International Criminal Court (ICC).
    • Secretary of State Marco Rubio emphasized that regular engagement among the three nuclear powers is essential to set a conducive tone for diplomacy and global safety.
    • The three nations also agreed to increase oil, gasoline, and diesel production to lower global prices.
  • Russia-Ukraine & Iran: Trump stated Putin wants to end the war in Ukraine diplomatically, noting an average of 25,933 people (mostly soldiers) are dying monthly, though animosity between Putin and President Zelenskyy remains a major hurdle. Regarding Iran, Trump stated the U.S. Navy has a blockade in the Hormuz Strait, took out record amounts of oil last week, and vowed Iran will never have a nuclear weapon.
  • Historic Travel & Regional Positioning: Air Force One completed a record 14.5-hour non-stop flight home without refueling (4 hours short of the previous record, compared to a typical 20-hour flight). Trump also noted the U.S. secured Greenland (thanking Denmark and NATO's Mark Rutte) and boxed China and Russia out of Venezuela and the Panama Canal.

Trade Deals & International Partnerships

  • ASEAN & Philippines ($17B+ in Deals): Addressing 14 ASEAN nations in Manila, Trump secured over $17 billion in commercial deals, including Philippine Airlines purchasing 20 Boeing 787 Dreamliners with General Electric engines (supporting 47,000 U.S. jobs).
    • The Philippines and Malaysia agreed to use U.S. cloud infrastructure.
    • Starting January 1, the Philippines will buy 100% of its agricultural products and 100% of its energy from the U.S., alongside over $2 billion in U.S. military equipment, under a framework deal slashing Philippine tariffs.
  • Thailand: Signed an agreement to eliminate all tariffs and non-tariff barriers on U.S. products and purchase $2.6 billion per year in U.S. agricultural goods.
  • Quad Summit (US, India, Australia, Japan): Launched the Quad Bureau leaders forum (meeting early next year), a Quad Super Intelligence (SI) accelerator, and new maritime security and surveillance collaborations.
  • China, Mexico & Canada: Trump said the U.S. is negotiating a "comprehensive" deal with China, noting previous tariffs reached 145% plus a 20% fentanyl penalty (fentanyl entering by sea is down 97%). He is also working with Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum on trade surpluses and cartels, and pressing Canada over an $80 billion trade deficit despite Prime Minister Carney's interest in building the Keystone pipeline.

Data Centers, AI & Tech Policy

  • Tax Threat on Foreign Data Centers: Trump announced plans to impose a "tremendous tax" and penalties on U.S. companies that build data centers in other countries (such as Finland) to prevent China from dominating the sector.
    • He urged communities in states like Texas, California, and West Virginia to welcome data centers, noting they generate their own electricity and offer local payouts—citing examples of companies paying teachers $50,000 in Louisiana and residents $10,000 each for project approvals.
  • Chips & Nvidia: Trump stated the U.S. is 10 years ahead of China in chipmaking and will only allow older chips—not the latest Blackwell chips—to be sold to China. He praised former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang as "brilliant" and said he would like to see Huang apologize and return to Nvidia.
  • AI Leadership: Trump noted Anthropic's recent successful launch, expressed respect for CEO Dario Amodei, and said he and President Xi agreed to establish joint guardrails on "super intelligence."

U.S. Economy, Infrastructure & Domestic Agenda

  • Economic Metrics & Manufacturing: Trump reported creating $25 trillion in new wealth in under two years (though $4 trillion was lost post-election), 10 straight months of manufacturing growth, a 21% increase in new equipment investment, and record-low poverty.
    • Crude oil is down 20% since November to ~$80/barrel, and gasoline is down 60 cents/gallon in the past month.
    • Trump criticized the Federal Reserve as "very political," arguing lower interest rates (where each point equals $650 billion) could push U.S. GDP from 4% to 14–20%.
    • Industrial Expansion: Audi ($4 billion plant), Toyota ($5 billion plant), GM, and Ford are expanding U.S. operations, including a 1906 Detroit Ford plant now running 24/7. Defense contractors are also building 21 plants for Patriots, Tomahawks, and THAAD systems.
  • Permitting Reform & ATC Overhaul: Vice President JD Vance highlighted the Senate's passage of bipartisan permitting reform legislation, which Trump said is projected to put at least $1,300/year back into Americans' pockets. Trump also requested $50 billion from Congress (up from $12 billion) to replace the 55-year-old air traffic control system under a single primary contractor like Raytheon or IBM.
  • Crime, Border & Bipartisan Bills: Trump reported zero illegal border crossings in 18 months, murders at a 125-year low, and crime down 87% in Washington, D.C. and 81% in Memphis. He expressed openness to working with Democrats on healthcare, immigration, a federal film/TV production bill, and an NCAA/NIL college sports bill (noting colleges are losing up to $500 million/year).
  • Administration Backing: Trump affirmed confidence in Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, citing tens of billions made on Intel stock. Chief of Staff Stephen Miller touted 10-to-1 net deregulation, ending DEI, record fossil fuel/coal production, and defeating 20%+ year-over-year inflation.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Economic & Legislative Impact: The bipartisan permitting reform bill, if passed by the House, is projected to put at least $1,300 per year back into the pockets of hardworking Americans — *President Donald Trump*.
  • Nuclear Parity: China is currently behind the U.S. and Russia with 600 nuclear weapons, but will catch up to the U.S. within 10 years — *President Donald Trump*.
  • Chip Obsolescence: Nvidia's latest Blackwell chips will be obsolete in two years — *President Donald Trump*.
  • College Sports: Every college in the country will go bankrupt if Congress does not pass an NIL/college sports reform bill — *President Donald Trump*.
  • Administration Outlook: Under President Trump's leadership, the next two years are going to be the greatest two years in American history — *Chief of Staff Stephen Miller*.

Notable Quotes

"We're not going to allow companies to build data centers in other countries. If they do that, they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them if they do it." — *President Donald Trump*
"The idea that these three leaders [the U.S., Russia, and China] should not engage on a regular basis is not just absurd, it's dangerous." — *Secretary of State Marco Rubio*
"More than anything, I think the President has done... is that he's reorganized the global economy so that it makes sense to invest in the United States of America again." — *Vice President JD Vance*
"The first President in American history to achieve net deregulation. In other words, not only were more regulations removed than added, but 10 to 1, for every new one, 10 regulations removed." — *Chief of Staff Stephen Miller*

Washington Today (11-20-26): Pres. Trump holds Asia trip news conf | Senate passes permitting reform

C-SPAN(2.0M)·59:46·16.5K views·2026-11-20
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Josh ShapiroGovernor, Pennsylvania Chuck SchumerSenate Minority Leader, United States
TL;DRPresident Donald Trump touted his historic trilateral summit with China and Russia and praised the Senate's passage of a sweeping bipartisan permitting reform bill, while Democrats sharply criticized his foreign policy and chip export approvals, and Congress moved to ban "death bets" on prediction markets and expedite House sexual misconduct investigations.
Trilateral Summit: President Donald Trump described his Asia trip as one of the most successful in U.S. history, noting he met with more than 20 world leaders in four days, secured two new trade deals, and has roughly 10 others coming soon. He highlighted the first-ever...

TL;DR: President Donald Trump touted his historic trilateral summit with China and Russia and praised the Senate's passage of a sweeping bipartisan permitting reform bill, while Democrats sharply criticized his foreign policy and chip export approvals, and Congress moved to ban "death bets" on prediction markets and expedite House sexual misconduct investigations.

Trump's Asia Trip, Russia, and U.S.-China Chip Policy

  • Trilateral Summit: President Donald Trump described his Asia trip as one of the most successful in U.S. history, noting he met with more than 20 world leaders in four days, secured two new trade deals, and has roughly 10 others coming soon.
    • He highlighted the first-ever trilateral meeting between the U.S., Russia, and China—noting the three nations hold 92% of the world's nuclear power—and stated they discussed preventing Iran from acquiring nuclear weapons, confronting dangers on the Korean Peninsula, and ending the war in Ukraine.
  • Relationship with Vladimir Putin: Trump defended calling Russian President Vladimir Putin a "friend," saying having a good relationship with the leader of a country with at least 3,000 nuclear weapons makes the U.S. safer.
    • *Ukraine Peace Prospects:* Trump argued both Putin and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy want to end the five-year war, though "the personalities play" and both are "stuck in mud." Meanwhile, the *Kyiv Post* reported that Russian Deputy Foreign Minister Sergey Ryabkov rejected providing Ukraine with NATO Article 5-style security guarantees, contradicting U.S. Envoy Jared Kushner.
  • AI Chip Exports & China Competition: Trump stated the U.S. wants only older chips sold to China, not advanced ones like Nvidia's latest Blackwell chip, though he noted the U.S. is 10 years ahead of China in chip-making and is discussing a "very comprehensive deal."
    • *Democratic Pushback:* Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer (D-NY) criticized Trump on the Senate floor, citing House Foreign Affairs Committee documents showing the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies (including Alibaba and Tencent) as of May 2026—including tens of thousands to firms tied to China's People's Liberation Army and a company affiliated with a suspected Chinese spy linked to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Schumer urged passage of the AI Overwatch Act, the MATCH Act, and the Chips Security Act.
    • *Foreign Policy Critique:* Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro (D-PA) argued at an Atlantic Council event that Trump "looked profoundly and pathetically weak with Putin," accusing him of undermining Ukraine and European allies while acting like a "junior partner" to Xi and Putin.
    • *House China Committee Hearing:* Rep. John Moolenaar (R-MI) noted China accounted for 72% of all new mature node chip capacity built worldwide between 2015 and 2023, while USTR set a 0% tariff on Chinese semiconductors until June 2027. Witness Pat Gelsinger (Playground Global) warned that delaying action digs a deeper hole and urged aggressive tariffs and restrictions on older DUV tools, while First Solar CEO Mark Widmar called for a bipartisan industrial strategy.

Bipartisan Permitting Reform & Senate Action

  • Permitting Reform Passes (62–33): The Senate passed the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAJA), crafted by Sens. Shelley Moore Capito (R-WV), Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI), Mike Lee (R-UT), and Martin Heinrich (D-NM), to speed up environmental reviews, accelerate electricity transmission, and restrict agencies from revoking existing permits.
    • *Support:* Trump praised the bill, projecting it will put at least $1,300 a year back into Americans' pockets and boost construction of data centers and "super intelligence" (SI) plants. House Majority Leader Steve Scalise (R-LA) urged the House to pass it. Outgoing Sen. Allen Armstrong (R-OK), who is succeeded by Sen.-elect Kevin Hern on Nov. 30, praised the bill in his farewell speech.
    • *Opposition:* Sens. Jeff Merkley (D-OR) and Ron Wyden (D-OR) opposed the bill after their amendments—addressing tribal consultation, state clean air/water input, public lands, and tariffs on wind components—were blocked.
  • Banning "Death Bets" on Prediction Markets: The Senate passed the Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy and Harm, Betting in Event Trading Systems (DEATH BETS) Act by unanimous consent.
    • Sponsored by Sens. Adam Schiff (D-CA) and John Curtis (R-UT), the bill prohibits prediction markets like Kalshi and Polymarket from offering contracts tied to war, U.S. military operations, terrorism, assassination, or an individual's death.

White House Leadership, Lame-Duck Gridlock & House Ethics

  • Stephen Miller's Debut: Making his first public remarks as White House Chief of Staff (having replaced Susie Wiles on Nov. 5), Stephen Miller praised Trump's first two years, claiming he achieved a 10-to-1 net deregulation ratio, secured the border, defeated 20%+ year-over-year inflation, and "took over Venezuela in less than an hour, doubling U.S. oil reserves overnight."
  • Working with a Democratic Congress: Trump said he plans to use his veto power to negotiate with incoming Speaker Hakeem Jeffries and Majority Leader Chuck Schumer, particularly on healthcare, suggesting he might get more done with Democrats than with certain "impossible" Republicans.
  • Lame-Duck Attendance & Holds: House Speaker Mike Johnson (R-LA) warned GOP members that absences are stalling legislation, while Rep. Thomas Massie (R-KY) noted on CNN that Republicans are "technically already in a minority" on the floor due to the "retirement caucus" looking for jobs. Meanwhile, Sen. Thom Tillis (R-NC) placed holds on nominees Eric Ueland (GAO) and Michael Opassio (CISA).
  • House Ethics Rule Changes: *The Hill* congressional reporter Sudiksha Kochi detailed the House Ethics Committee's bipartisan vote to grant leadership subpoena authority, streamline deadlines, and publicly disclose findings of sexual misconduct. Kochi noted the changes follow high-profile cases involving Reps. Cory Mills, Jimmy Gomez, and former Reps. Tony Gonzales and Eric Swalwell.

International & Local Developments

  • UK-EU Summit: Marking a decade since Brexit, British Prime Minister Andy Burnham and the EU agreed to remove trade barriers and expand youth travel, though opposition Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch criticized the deal for allowing up to 100,000 young people per year into the UK.
  • Market & Local News: The Dow fell 949 points, the Nasdaq dropped 1,261, and the S&P 500 fell 265, erasing 2026 gains amid an AI rout. NYC Mayor Eric Adams pushed back the Rikers Island jail closure from 2027 to 2032 while on crutches after tearing his Achilles in a soccer game. Sen. Bernie Moreno (R-OH) honored Fiona, the Cincinnati Zoo hippopotamus who died after swallowing a visitor's hat, and the 76-foot Engelmann spruce Capitol Christmas tree arrived from Idaho.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Blackwell Chip Obsolescence: "In two years, Blackwell will be obsolete, and you care much less about it." — President Donald Trump.
  • Permitting Reform Savings: The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act is "projected to put at least $1,300 a year back in the pockets of hardworking Americans." — President Donald Trump.
  • White House & Democratic Congress: "They're going to send things my way and I'm going to veto them... [for] two years and one month... But I think after a month or so... there's a real possibility that we get a lot of great things done, especially on healthcare." — President Donald Trump.
  • House Ethics & Senate Outlook: "I don't really expect the Senate to take up some of these [sexual misconduct] reforms, although I do expect with Democrats taking control of the chamber to push for some of this." — Sudiksha Kochi, *The Hill*.
  • UK-EU Relations: "What we will seek to do over the next year before the next UK, EU, summit, perhaps around this time next year, we will seek to conduct an informal brainstorming process... to look at what those options are." — British Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

Notable Quotes

"I have fantastic relationships with Putin, that's a very good thing. I have fantastic relationships with President Xi, that's a good thing, and our country is safe because of that." — President Donald Trump
"Trump needs to realize that the only reason he received such a warm welcome from the Chinese Communist Party this week was because he's doling out our most advanced AI chips to China like they're party favors." — Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer (D-NY)
"Betting and profiting off of death, both in tragedy and in war, is immoral and ought to be unlawful." — Sen. Adam Schiff (D-CA)
"The next two years are going to be the greatest two years in American history." — White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller

I came to Washington with a conviction that our country had made it far too difficult to build the things we need. For nearly 40 years, I had worked alongside the engineers, the construction workers, the environmental specialists, and entrepreneurs who build the infrastructure our economy and our national security depends on. I had seen firsthand what Americans can accomplish when they're given the opportunity, but I had also watched with growing frustration as our permitting system became increasingly complicated, unpredictable, and very difficult to navigate. Projects that should have taken a year were taking a decade. Investments that could have created jobs and strengthened our economy were delayed or abandoned. I believe we could establish a fair, more predictable permitting process without compromising environmental protections or creating other federal spending programs. Because permitting reform isn't just about pipelines, power lines, or factories, it's actually about the people who depend on those services.
