Why AI Has DHH Working 16-Hour Days

a16z(312.0K)·52:16·63.2K views·2026-11-20
David Heinemeier HanssonCreator, Omarchy Martin CasadoGeneral Partner, a16z Anish AcharyaGeneral Partner, a16z
TL;DRDavid Heinemeier Hansson (DHH) argues that the agent era has reopened the operating system market for the first time in 25 years, making Linux—whose terminal, config files, and detailed error messages are ideal for AI—the natural successor to macOS and Windows.
Through his agent-first Linux distribution, Omarchy, DHH demonstrates how tiny teams can now rival tech giants, while taste, craft, and the obsession over the "final 2%" remain the true differentiators. The End of Mouse-and-Click OSs: DHH, who spent 30 years building software...

TL;DR

David Heinemeier Hansson (DHH) argues that the agent era has reopened the operating system market for the first time in 25 years, making Linux—whose terminal, config files, and detailed error messages are ideal for AI—the natural successor to macOS and Windows. Through his agent-first Linux distribution, Omarchy, DHH demonstrates how tiny teams can now rival tech giants, while taste, craft, and the obsession over the "final 2%" remain the true differentiators.

The Agent-First OS Opportunity: Why Linux Wins Now

  • The End of Mouse-and-Click OSs: DHH, who spent 30 years building software (Basecamp v5, Ruby on Rails v8, Hey.com) and used Mac since OS X Beta 4 in 2001, switched to Ubuntu 2.5 years ago over Apple’s 30% App Store cut and developer hostility. He argues macOS and Windows are fundamentally unfit for agents, whereas Linux’s historic weaknesses—heavy terminal use, text config files, and verbose error messages—are the exact interfaces agents love.
  • Omarchy’s Rapid Rise: Built part-time in ~2.5 months by DHH and Ryan Hughes, Omarchy is designed to be modified live by prompting. DHH has not written a single line of code by hand in 5–6 months, instead generating hundreds of thousands of lines via agents. The Omarchy Foundation has raised ~$23M in tokens and donations.
  • Breaking Platform Lock-In:
    • Desktop & Mobile: Apple risks becoming the "AOL of the agent era" with walled gardens and features like Genmoji. DHH’s motto is *"first we take Manhattan (desktop), then we take Berlin (mobile)."*
    • Eradicating App Moats: An Omarchy community member replicated Adobe Lightroom in two weeks—a feat that took Adobe ~15 years and tens of millions of dollars. DHH no longer uses Word or Photoshop, relying on agents to draft contracts, generate PDFs, sign documents, and edit photos.
    • Anish Acharya’s Take: Five years ago, building a third mobile ecosystem was certain to fail due to app availability; today, a modest token budget can replicate the top 10,000 apps, or agents can simply perform the work natively without the app.

The Economics of Software, Speed, and the Final 2%

  • Token Velocity & Model Leaps: Current models run at ~50 tokens/sec (120 on ultra-fast), but Fireworks recently demonstrated 1,500 tokens/sec—generating a playable Tetris game in 4 seconds and mutating a side-scroller shooter live. During Omarchy’s development, models jumped from Opus 48 to Saul to Opus 55.
  • Negligible Cost: Despite having the means for unlimited compute, DHH only uses three Claude subscriptions—and mostly relies on a single $200 20x subscription, maxing it out only two weeks out of the last three months.
  • Craftsmanship & Taste Still Matter: Martin Casado noted that great software—like *StarCraft* receiving a Ghost balance patch after 20 years—is defined by long-tail design choices rather than raw lines of code. DHH agreed, explaining he is working 16-hour days because agents eliminate the friction of fixing the "final 2%," where he gets his dopamine.
  • Hyperdrive Without Headcount: Basecamp/37signals has ~60 employees; DHH never wanted 6,000 or 60,000. Agents provide the productive capacity of 60,000 people with zero HR or coordination overhead—echoing Shopify CEO Tobi Lütke’s description of returning to a 7,000-person office as "coming out of hyperdrive."

Superintelligence, Frontier Labs vs. Open Weights

  • Software vs. Hardware Timelines: DHH believes we already have superintelligence for software development. However, Martin Casado’s personal AGI test—giving models a $20,000 budget to order parts and build an autonomous, walkable robot—shows physical embodiment is far behind (his setup, Groqbot, mostly orders home automation gear). Visiting Unitree in China two weeks prior, DHH found robotics engineers projecting 4–10 years for embodied breakthroughs, contrasting with Silicon Valley’s 1-year timelines.
  • Avoiding a Frontier Duopoly: Drawing parallels to the 1990s encryption wars and Google/Meta capturing 99% of online ad value, DHH warned against a commercial or government-enforced duopoly between top labs (Anthropic and OpenAI).
  • The Role of Open Weights: DHH cheers on US frontier labs (including xAI and Google) while stressing that open-weight models (e.g., GLM 53, DeepSeek Flash, which he estimates are ~6–7 months behind) keep pricing honest. Local models like Qwen 3 8 Flash/Next now run usefully on a single-digit-thousand-dollar DGX Spark on a desk.

The Return of Hacker, Demo, and Pseudo-Anonymous Culture

  • Industry Veterans & Empathy: Martin Casado and Anish Acharya highlighted how stalwarts like DHH, Eric Raymond, John Carmack, Tim Sweeney, and Mitchell Hashimoto are guiding the industry. While DHH expressed empathy for anxious programmers—comparing them to 1970s Detroit auto workers and English Luddites—he insisted there is no going back.
  • Pseudo-Anonymous Builders: Agents are redemocratizing software, bringing back 1990s hacker culture. The Omarchy core team and Foundation sponsor pseudo-anonymous contributors like Outfoxed (creator of QuickShell), Handcore, 0x0, and MiasLab, connected largely via X.
  • 90s Demo & BBS Nostalgia: Rejecting Jony Ive’s "aluminum-polished" aesthetic that stripped character from computers, Omarchy embraces 1995 demo-party vibes with a 40-effect ASCII screensaver and ASCII loading animations. DHH reminisced about running an illegal 3-line BBS out of his bedroom at age 14 on a US Robotics 33.6k modem, arguing computers should feel mysterious and cool again.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Unseating macOS: Omarchy/Linux will surpass macOS’s 180 million users within 3 years—and potentially within 12 months, though DHH wouldn't put majority odds on the 1-year mark. *(DHH)*
  • True Superintelligence: It is "entirely possible" that true superintelligence—better than humans at everything, including polishing a bespoke Linux kernel—arrives by next year. *(DHH)*
  • Macroeconomic Impact: Widespread adoption of current agentic workflows puts annual GDP acceleration of 3%, 4%, 5%, or even 10% within the realm of possibility. *(DHH)*
  • Embodied Robotics: Physical, actuator-driven AI breakthroughs are likely 4, 5, or 10 years away rather than imminent. *(Unitree engineers, relayed by DHH)*

Notable Quotes

"Every single thing that made Linux hard to use in the past—oh, it's very terminal heavy, it's got a bunch of config files that's hard to figure out, and it's got inscrutable error messages—these are the three things agents love." — David Heinemeier Hansson
"Anyone who still thinks that there is moat in source code, even millions of lines of it, or that that is going to protect their ecosystem are delusional." — David Heinemeier Hansson

"Computers should be a little mysterious, they should be a lot of cool, and they should not be so polished... We're fucking getting the hackers back." — David Heinemeier Hansson

Now I've been using Linux on the server since the 90s, yeah, yeah, but I did not run Linux on the desktop. Well, what was not golden about that was their relationship to developers and I was certainly one of those developers who had been advocated for Apple for decades, suddenly, finally, I had enough and I switched to Linux. And there was just another drop today that Apple is making it even harder to use macOS with agents, even more scary pop-up screens about how you shouldn't let Cloud have access to your files. Well, what is it going to do then if it doesn't have access to your files? So, Omachi is just going in a very different direction. We are leaning all in, the entire operating system is being developed by agents for the past five months or so now, maybe even six months. I've not written a single line of code for Omachi by hand. But I've written hundreds of thousands of lines of code through agents. And we're using that to accelerate the development of Omachi itself and we're creating things at a rate I scarcely believe would be possible, and then we're putting all that power into the hands of users. And I think exactly as you had, anyone who gets the experience where the entire operating system is malleable, that they can change anything, they can change how any element of it work, they want a new start menu, they want to move the clock around, they want a different calendar, they just want to add a feature to an existing part of the operating system and they can just do that by prompting and it literally happens in sometimes seconds, maybe at most a few minutes, is deeply intoxicating, and I don't think any of those people, if they really soak in it for 5 minutes, are going back. So I'm extremely bullish on the idea that both macOS and Windows are just unfit for the age of agents and I think the age of agents is going to be the only thing that matters when it comes to these computers in about 5 minutes. So we're really just leaning hard into the idea that operating systems we've had for 40 years built around mouse and click dynamics, they just don't work for the age of agents. I mean, let me give you just one basic example. If you want to remap the hotkeys to go to a different workspace in macOS, there's only one way to do that, manually go into a settings app, click one field at a time, put in the new hotkey then click the next field then put in that hotkey, you cannot do that identically. Well, I guess you can have computer users do it, but that's pretty dumb, that's pretty silly. And what we have on the Linux side is that every single thing that made Linux hard to use in the past, oh, it's very terminal heavy, it's got a bunch of config files that's hard to figure out and it's got inscrutable error messages. These are the three things agents love, they want a command line interface for everything, they want predictable config files that they can just edit and update, they want all the details in the air messages, so all these historic deficits for Linux have literally this year turned into the reason why you want Linux on your computer, if you're into agents, if you're into AI. And at this point, who that's making anything is not into agents, is not into AI? So I think Apple is running the risk right now of becoming the AOL of the agent era. We're going to provide this walled garden, we have Genmoji over here, you do not need to use an actual agent from an actual Frontier lab, just give us a chance to give you something watered down and protected and most people don't want AOL, they want the open internet and this is what we're trying to give them with Omachi. What happens to mobile in this world? Mobile has to change the same way. And this is actually one of the reasons why I am so excited about this age of agents. First on the desktop, because the window is now open for a third operating system for the first time in 25 years. The last time we had an opening was that OS 10 in 2001, that was the last time anyone premiered a new operating system that made any dent in the global market share. Since then, nothing. Windows closed, this year, window open, suddenly Linux has a chance again in a way it literally hasn't happened in 25 years. Mobile has been suffering under the same setup, we've had a duopoly and Google and Apple have just divided that juicy 30% revenue from everyone else's business between them, and what were you going to do? Microsoft tried, they spent I don't know, several billion dollars to make Windows OS a thing on mobile and it wasn't a thing, in huge part because they did not have the applications, who wanted to switch to a Windows phone if they couldn't get Instagram. So that died on the vine. That's what's changing right now, we're seeing it already on Linux.
